chronological order. Do not edit or delete entries.

Enriched strategy hubs; cross to Luttwak 2002, Roman controversy, Byzantine empire actor, periods. Source + .txt filed to raw/3. Middle Ages/. Outstanding War Strategy Sources ✅. Co-Authored-By: Claude Code (xAI).

Format:
- Ingest: `## [YYYY-MM-DD] ingest | [Source Title] | [Period(s)] | [Region(s)] | [Pages created: N] | [Pages updated: N]`
- Query: `## [YYYY-MM-DD] query | [Question summary]`
- Lint: `## [YYYY-MM-DD] lint | [Issues found: N] | [Issues resolved: N]`

Key claims from all ranges: HG "state of nature" (15–35% violent male death; raids/ambushes primary); agriculture/sedentism scaled war; state as war "spin-off" enabling civilization; steppe horse-nomad predatory raiding (mobility, composite bow, extortion model, Great Wall); shift raid→siege/battle; decline in per-capita violence under state; persistence/transformation of evolutionary motivations (Red Queen, kin altruism expansion, status/power/honour); modern affluent liberal democracies + nuclear weapons flip the calculus (Kantian peace: democracy + interdependence + IOs; conquest unprofitable; liberal superiority in total war but constraints in small wars); critiques of Mead (cultural invention), realists (status-quo bias), Mueller. Remaining risks in non-liberal zones, terror, WMD. Full Range 5 (conclusion/endnotes) incorporated. Enriched warfare/strategy hubs and new concept/process anchors. Source + .txt filed to raw/4. Modern Times/. Outstanding roadmap updated. Co-Authored-By: Claude Code (xAI).

peasants responded to administrative measures by waiting for higher grain prices... chose to store their grain, feed it to their livestock or illicitly convert it into alcohol ll.18541; proportion of women in industry rose from 27 per cent in 1914 to 43 per cent in 1917 ll.18565; Pelling's narrative from the triumvirate and Philippi (42) through the breakdown of Antony and Octavian, Actium (31), and the fall of Alexandria (30) — the end of the civil wars and the Republic.

## 2026-06-11 phase-start | The Cambridge Ancient History Vol. IX — The Last Age of the Roman Republic, 146–43 B.C. (Crook, Lintott & Rawson eds., 2nd ed. 1992) | Large-Volume Protocol | Classical Antiquity (146–43 BCE) | source page crook-cah-v9-1992.md written with 8-cycle Section Plan. The direct sequel to CAH VIII: the Fall of the Roman Republic. Two-part structure (narrative chs 1–12; thematic chs 13–19 + Epilogue). Begins at 146 (Carthage & Corinth) to frame the Gracchi (133); ends at the death of Cicero (43). Picks up the agrarian crisis, the allies' question, and the eroded cursus conventions laid down in CAH VIII.

## 2026-06-11 ingest-complete | The Cambridge Ancient History Vol. VIII — Rome and the Mediterranean to 133 B.C. (Astin, Walbank, Frederiksen & Ogilvie eds., 2nd ed. 1989) | Total pages created: 41 | Total pages updated: 17 (distinct pre-existing pages) | Sections processed: 6 | Step 3 synthesis pass done — Volume Synthesis Note written on sources/astin-cah-v8-1989.md (the realization of Polybius' symploke; Rome's empire and internal transformation as one process; the Harris-vs-defensive imperialism axis; the Hellenistic kingdoms in their own terms; the cultural symploke; the hand-off to CAH IX). Step 4: source already converted markdown in raw/Markdown/ (no PDF to convert/delete), consistent with CAH II.1/II.2/V/VI/VII.1/VII.2; left in place. index.md updated — CAH VIII source row added, header refreshed, Summary Statistics recomputed by on-disk file count (Periods 24, Events 113, Processes 84, Actors 260, Places 87, Concepts 123, Controversies 33, Sources 20; Total 744). This volume completes the Phase-2 Greco-Roman backbone (CAH I–VIII): the wiki now holds a continuous narrative from the prehistoric Near East through the Roman conquest of the Mediterranean to the eve of the Gracchi. It gave the wiki the Hannibalic War, the conquest of Spain and the destruction of Carthage, the standing controversy on the nature of Roman imperialism, the mid-Republican political system and the building agrarian/allies crisis, the Roman conquest of the Greek East (Cynoscephalae, Apamea, Pydna, the sack of Corinth) and the "freedom of the Greeks," the Hellenistic East in its own decline (Antiochus IV, the Maccabees, the Indo-Greeks), and the Hellenization of Roman culture. Next backbone volume in Processing-List order: CAH IX — The Last Age of the Roman Republic 146–43 BC.

## 2026-06-11 section | CAH Vol. VIII | Section: Cycle 6 — Hellenization & material transformation (Chs 12–13: Rawson on Roman tradition & the Greek world; Morel on the archaeology of Italy 300–133) | Pages created: 1 | Pages updated: 1 | Created processes/hellenization-of-rome (the cultural & material absorption of Greek civilisation: the literary awakening — Livius Andronicus, Naevius, Ennius, Plautus, Cato; the adapted import of Greek religion via the Sibylline books — the lectisternium, Apollo's games, Magna Mater; Morel's archaeology of the material transformation — the villa economy, black-glaze trade, Greek architecture, Rome as a Hellenistic city; Rawson's "reaction and acceptance" — against a simple philhellene/hellenophobe split, with Cato as the central ambivalent figure; "the conqueror was never taken wholly captive"). Updated actors/cato-the-elder (Rawson's reading of his cultural ambivalence). The cultural counterpart of the political symploke — the fusion of the Greek and Roman worlds, the formative first phase of the Hellenization that would define late-Republican and imperial culture.

## 2026-06-11 section | CAH Vol. VIII | Section: Cycle 5 — The Hellenistic East in its own right (Chs 10–11: Habicht on the Seleucids & their rivals; Narain on the Greeks of Bactria & India) | Pages created: 4 | Pages updated: 3 | Created actors/antiochus-iv-epiphanes (the energetic king; Habicht's rehabilitation against the "Epimanes" caricature and the forced-Hellenization thesis), events/day-of-eleusis-168bce (Popillius Laenas' circle in the sand — Rome dictating to the East without an army), events/maccabean-revolt-167-160bce (Habicht's revisionist account: the Hellenizing initiative from the Jewish priesthood itself; Antiochus' misreading; the Temple rededication 164 → Hasmonean state), actors/menander-i (the Indo-Greek king "Milinda" of the Milindapañha; deepest Greek reach into India). Note: Ch. 10 §I (Attalid apogee, Rome's rebuff of Eumenes after Pydna, Rhodes' humiliation) and the last-Attalids/province-of-Asia material largely updated the pergamon & rhodes pages already extended in Cycle 4. Habicht's portrait of the Hellenistic kingdoms in their own terms — declining under the long shadow of Rome — and Narain's recovery of the Indo-Greeks from their coinage.

## 2026-06-11 section | CAH Vol. VIII | Section: Cycle 4 — Conquest of the Greek East (Chs 8–9: Errington on Rome vs Philip & Antiochus; Derow on the fall of Macedon & the sack of Corinth) | Pages created: 9 | Pages updated: 6 | Created events/second-macedonian-war-200-196bce (Cynoscephalae 197; the Isthmian proclamation 196), events/roman-syrian-war-192-188bce (Thermopylae 191, Magnesia 190), events/peace-of-apamea-188bce (Seleucids confined behind the Taurus; Pergamon & Rhodes doubled), events/third-macedonian-war-171-168bce (Perseus, Pydna 168, the four republics, the deportations), events/achaean-war-146bce (the sack of Corinth, end of Greek freedom); actors/flamininus, perseus-of-macedon, aemilius-paullus; concepts/freedom-of-the-greeks (the slogan as instrument of indirect control sliding into the demand for obedience — Callicrates 180); places/corinth. Updated actors/antiochus-iii-the-great (the Roman war & Apamea), actors/achaean-league (Callicrates, the deportation of Polybius, the war of 146), actors/pergamon (Attalus I/Eumenes II, Apamea gains, then Roman suspicion), actors/rhodes (Apamea gains, then the post-Pydna humiliation & Delos free port), actors/antigonid-dynasty (the end of the dynasty at Pydna), actors/philip-v (post-Apamea humiliation & death 179). Errington's reading of Roman diplomacy-by-ultimatum and the instrumental "freedom of the Greeks," and Derow's account of Roman imperium as the demand for obedience — the arc from Cynoscephalae (197) through Apamea (188) and Pydna (168) to the destruction of Corinth (146), the realization of Polybius' symploke and the extinction of Greek autonomy.

## 2026-06-11 section | CAH Vol. VIII | Section: Cycle 3 — Roman government, society & Italy (Chs 6–7: Astin on government & politics 200–134; Gabba on Rome and Italy in the 2nd c.) | Pages created: 4 | Pages updated: 1 | Created concepts/roman-aristocratic-politics (the mid-Republican system: Polybius' mixed constitution with the Senate central; the Gelzer/Münzer/Scullard factional model and Astin's/Brunt's/Millar's critique; nobilitas, clientela, the cursus honorum & lex Villia annalis 180, mores & sumptuary laws, the trials of the Scipios; the forces eroding it toward 133 — the ballot laws, recruiting crises, Scipio Aemilianus' illegal consulships), processes/agrarian-crisis-roman-republic (the post-Hannibalic ager publicus, conquest wealth, slave latifundia, depopulation & long conscription displacing the peasant-soldier; the law de modo agrorum 500 iugera; the road to the Gracchan land reform of 133), concepts/roman-italian-alliance (the socii system; the Hannibalic rupture at Cannae; confiscation & maiestas populi Romani; the allies transformed from junior partners into imperial substructure; the deferred citizenship question → Social War), actors/cato-the-elder (the self-regulating novus homo — censor 184, De agri cultura, Origines, Carthago delenda). Updated actors/roman-republic (the crisis of 133 as the interlock of agrarian crisis, eroded oligarchic conventions, and the allies' grievance). Astin's anatomy of the competitive oligarchy operating through popular forms, and Gabba's account of the agrarian transformation and the allies' subordination — together the structural background to the Gracchan cataclysm reserved for CAH IX.

## 2026-06-11 section | CAH Vol. VIII | Section: Cycle 2 — Expansion West & first Eastern contacts (Chs 4–5: Errington on Rome and Greece to 205; Harris on Roman expansion in the west) | Pages created: 8 | Pages updated: 5 | Created events/illyrian-wars-229-219bce (Rome's first trans-Adriatic intervention; the Polybius-vs-Appian source problem), events/first-macedonian-war-214-205bce (the Hannibal–Philip treaty, the Aetolian alliance, Peace of Phoenice), events/third-punic-war-149-146bce (the deliberate destruction of Carthage; Harris's causation), events/numantine-war-153-133bce (the Celtiberian "fiery war" to the fall of Numantia 133); actors/viriathus, actors/scipio-aemilianus (destroyer of Carthage & Numantia); controversies/nature-of-roman-imperialism (the Holleaux/Badian/Errington/Briscoe "defensive" vs Harris "aggressive/systemic" debate — the etic frame for all mid-Republican expansion); places/hispania (the two Spains, silver, the stipendium & repetundae). Updated actors/philip-v (First Macedonian War & Phoenice), actors/aetolian-league (Rome's first Greek ally; the separate peace of 206), concepts/roman-provincial-system (the Spanish provinces, the lex Calpurnia repetundae court of 149, the province of Africa 146), processes/roman-expansion-italy (the conquest of Cisalpine Gaul & Liguria as the northern coda), actors/carthage (the Third Punic War destruction & province of Africa). Covers Rome's reactive Adriatic policing (the two Illyrian Wars), the First Macedonian War as a Hannibalic-War sideshow drawing Rome into Greek politics, the relentless conquest of Cisalpine Gaul/Liguria and of Spain (Cato, the Celtiberian & Lusitanian wars, Viriathus, Numantia), and the cold-blooded destruction of Carthage — all read through Harris's thesis of systemic Roman bellicosity.

## 2026-06-11 section | CAH Vol. VIII | Section: Cycle 1 — Sources & the Hannibalic War (Chs 1–3: Astin on sources; Scullard on the Carthaginians in Spain; Briscoe on the Second Punic War) | Pages created: 13 | Pages updated: 4 | Created events/second-punic-war-218-201bce (the war anchor — Italy/Spain/Sicily/Africa, manpower & finance, allies), events/battle-of-cannae-216bce, events/battle-of-zama-202bce, events/siege-of-saguntum-219bce; actors/hannibal, scipio-africanus, hamilcar-barca, fabius-maximus, masinissa, livy; concepts/fabian-strategy; controversies/causes-of-second-punic-war (Polybius' three aitiai vs Fabian tradition; Saguntum/Ebro legal tangle; defensive-imperialism Briscoe vs aggressive-imperialism Harris); places/new-carthage. Updated actors/carthage (Barcid recovery + the war + peace of 201), actors/roman-republic (internal consequences — manpower, Senate ascendancy, the allies question, eastward turn), actors/polybius (principal source for 220–146; transmission via Livy), periods/classical-antiquity (the Hannibalic War as the supreme test of the mature Republic). The road to war (Barcid Spain, the Ebro treaty, Saguntum), Hannibal's invasion and the three disasters (Trebbia, Trasimene, Cannae), the Fabian attrition, the recovery of the defecting cities, Scipio's conquest of Spain and African campaign to Zama, and the war's structural lessons.

## 2026-06-11 phase-start | The Cambridge Ancient History Vol. VIII — Rome and the Mediterranean to 133 B.C. (Astin, Walbank, Frederiksen & Ogilvie eds., 2nd ed. 1989) | Large-Volume Protocol | Classical Antiquity (c. 220–133 BCE) | source page astin-cah-v8-1989.md written with 6-cycle Section Plan. The culminating volume of the Phase-2 Greco-Roman backbone: the Second Punic War, the Roman conquest of the Greek East, and Mediterranean hegemony to the eve of the Gracchi. Where VII.1 closed the East at Raphia (217) and VII.2 closed Rome at 220, Vol. VIII fuses the two — Polybius' symploke realized. Eastern actors (Philip V, Antiochus III, Seleucids, Attalids, Rhodes, the leagues) and Roman/Italian actors (Roman Republic, Carthage) already exist and will be updated forward.

Musti's Seleucid kingdom: a personal multi-ethnic military monarchy (king + Friends + dynameis); the laoi; viceroyalties & satrapies; the Raphia army; temple-states conserved not secularised; Seleucid vs Ptolemaic colonization; the Heuss/Bikerman city debate; the secession of Bactria (Diodotus) and Parthia (Andragoras + the Parni, era 247).

## 2026-06-11 section | CAH Vol. VII.1 | Section: Cycle 3 — Ptolemaic Egypt (Ch 5, Turner) | Pages created: 6 | Pages updated: 2 | Created actors/ptolemaic-egypt, actors/ptolemy-ii-philadelphus, places/alexandria (Museum, Library, Pharos, Alexandrian scholarship), concepts/ptolemaic-papyri (incl. the Zenon archive), concepts/sarapis, controversies/ptolemaic-economy (Rostovtzeff's "state socialism" vs the Préaux/Bingen/Turner pragmatic-fiscalism revision; the fusion question). Turner's revisionist chapter: the discontinuous papyrus base; the cleruchy and the royal economy (two models, the Revenue Laws as tax-farming not planning); "It was Philadelphus, not Philopator, who bankrupted Egypt"; interpenetration without fusion.

## 2026-06-11 ingest-complete | CAH Vol. V — The Fifth Century B.C. (Lewis, Boardman, Davies & Ostwald eds., 1992) | Total pages created: 33 | Total pages updated: 14 | Sections processed: 7 + synthesis

Large-Volume-Protocol ingest of CAH V complete. Seven section cycles carried the Greek fifth
century from the Persian-War aftermath to the fall of Athens (404): (1) sources/method &
Greece after the Persian Wars; (2) the Delian League → empire and the Athenian revolution
(Ephialtes/Pericles); (3) mainland Greece 479–451, the First Peloponnesian War & the Thirty
Years' Peace; (4) fifth-century Sicily (Hiero, the fall of the tyrannies, Ducetius, democratic
Syracuse); (5) the High Classical cultural chapter (art, the Parthenon, panhellenic games,
festivals, drama, society, the sophists); (6) the Archidamian War & the plague; (7) the Peace
of Nicias, the Sicilian Expedition, and the Spartan resurgence to Aegospotami and the surrender
of Athens. Step 3 synthesis pass done — Volume Synthesis Note written on
`sources/lewis-cah-v5-1992.md` (the double helix of political divergence and cultural
efflorescence; the empire-as-engine-of-democracy; the polis that could not hold a land
hegemony; the Athenocentric source-lens; the seamless joins with CAH IV behind and CAH VI
ahead). Step 4: the source is already converted markdown in `raw/Markdown/` (no PDF to
convert/delete), consistent with the filing of CAH II.1/II.2; left in place. `index.md` updated
— CAH V source row added, header refreshed, and Summary Statistics recomputed by on-disk file
count (Events 62, Actors 181, Places 78, Concepts 100, Sources 16; Total 563). This volume gave
the wiki its first dense narrative of the Athenian empire, the radical democracy, the
Peloponnesian War in all three phases, and the Classical cultural apex. Next backbone volume in
Processing-List order: CAH VI — The Fourth Century B.C. (converted in raw/Markdown/, un-ingested).

## 2026-06-11 section | CAH Vol. V (Lewis et al. 1992) | Cycle 7 — The Peace of Nicias & the Sicilian Expedition (Ch. 10) + The Spartan Resurgence (Ch. 11) (Andrewes) | Classical Antiquity | aegean, italy, anatolia | Pages created: 5 | Pages updated: 1

The final content cycle, carrying the Peloponnesian War to its end. Ch. 10 covers the failure
of the Peace of Nicias (the Argive/Quadruple Alliance, the Spartan victory at Mantinea 418
restoring prestige, the ostracism of Hyperbolus — the last ostracism), Melos and the Melian
Dialogue (416), and the Sicilian Expedition (415–413): Segesta's appeal, Alcibiades/Nicias/
Lamachus, the Hermae/Mysteries scandal and Alcibiades' recall & defection, the siege of
Syracuse, Gylippus's counter-wall, the Great Harbour naval defeats, the lunar-eclipse delay,
and the annihilation at the Assinarus (deaths of Nicias and Demosthenes). Ch. 11 covers the
Spartan resurgence: the occupation of Decelea, the Ionian War and Persian intervention
(Tissaphernes, Pharnabazus, the treaties, Alcibiades' intrigues), the oligarchic revolution of
the Four Hundred and Five Thousand (411), Cynossema and Cyzicus (410), the restored democracy,
Alcibiades' triumphant return (407), Notium (406), Arginusae and the trial of the generals
(406), Lysander and Cyrus, Aegospotami (405), and the surrender of Athens (404). Created:
`events/sicilian-expedition.md` (resolves `[[sicilian-expedition]]`),
`events/fall-of-athens-404bce.md` (resolves `[[fall-of-athens-404bce]]`; a transition page),
`events/siege-of-melos-416bce.md` (the Melian Dialogue), `actors/alcibiades.md`,
`actors/lysander.md`. Updated: `actors/syracuse.md` (the Sicilian Expedition repulse). All
content cycles complete; close-out (synthesis pass, index.md, final log, push) remains.

## 2026-06-11 section | CAH Vol. V (Lewis et al. 1992) | Cycle 6 — The Archidamian War (Ch. 9, Lewis) | Classical Antiquity | central-greece, peloponnese, aegean | Pages created: 5 | Pages updated: 1

The first phase of the Peloponnesian War (431–421). Ch. 9 covers the causes (Thucydides'
aitiai — Corcyra/Epidamnus, Potidaea, the Megarian Decree, Corinth — vs. the "truest cause,"
Spartan fear of Athenian power; the ananke/inevitability thesis), Periclean defensive strategy
(evacuate Attica, hold walls & fleet, raid the coast, keep allies/finance; the possible
hidden Megara aim), the plague (430, ~25% mortality) and Pericles' death (429), the war's
brutality and stasis (Corcyra), Plataea (fall 427), the Mytilenean revolt & debate (427),
Pylos/Sphacteria (425, the 120 Spartiates, the rejected peace), Brasidas in Thrace (the loss
of Amphipolis 424) and Delium (424), and the deaths of Cleon and Brasidas at Amphipolis (422)
leading to the Peace of Nicias (421). Created: `events/peloponnesian-war.md` (the major
overarching war page; resolves the wiki-wide `[[peloponnesian-war]]` link, with forward-links
to the Cycle-7 pages), `events/archidamian-war.md`, `events/plague-of-athens-430bce.md`,
`actors/cleon.md`, `actors/brasidas.md`. Updated: `actors/pericles.md` (war leadership,
strategy, Funeral Oration, plague death). Dangling links for Cycle 7: `[[sicilian-expedition]]`,
`[[fall-of-athens-404bce]]`; also still open: `archidamus-ii`, `[[corinth]]`.

## 2026-06-11 section | CAH Vol. V (Lewis et al. 1992) | Cycle 5 — Greek culture, religion & society (Ch. 8a–8h: Pollitt, Wycherley, Richardson, Burkert, Knox, Davies, Ostwald) | Classical Antiquity | central-greece, aegean | Pages created: 5 | Pages updated: 3

The volume's great composite cultural chapter, the editors' distinctive contribution (culture
in its historical/social/imperial context, not as a separate appendix). Ch. 8a (Pollitt):
art Archaic→Classical — late-Archaic naturalism & foreshortening, the Severe Style and the
Persian-War moral climate (Olympia), the High Classical Parthenon "dual nature," the
late-fifth-century perspective/wet-drapery turn, the artist as intellectual (Phidias,
Polyclitus' Canon). Chs. 8b–8c (Wycherley): the rebuilding of Athens and the Periclean
Acropolis (Parthenon, Propylaea, Erechtheum, Hephaesteum; Ictinus/Callicrates/Phidias). Ch. 8d
(Richardson): the panhellenic games & epinician poetry (Pindar). Ch. 8e (Burkert): Athenian
cults & festivals. Ch. 8f (Knox): tragedy (Aeschylus/Sophocles/Euripides) & Old Comedy
(Aristophanes) as fused religion-and-literature at the civic Dionysia. Ch. 8g (Davies):
society & economy. Ch. 8h (Ostwald): Athens as a cultural centre — the foreigners
(Herodotus, Hellanicus), the sophists (Protagoras, Gorgias, Prodicus, Hippias), the natural
philosophers (Anaxagoras, Archelaus), rhetoric, the nomos–physis antithesis, and science
(geometry, astronomy, Hippocratic medicine). Created: `concepts/classical-greek-art.md`,
`places/parthenon.md` (resolves the wiki-wide `[[parthenon]]` link), `concepts/athenian-drama.md`,
`concepts/sophists.md`, `concepts/panhellenic-games.md` (resolves `[[panhellenic-games]]`).
Updated: `places/athens-fifth-century.md` (cultural apex), `concepts/presocratics.md`
(philosophy reaches Athens; 1→2), `concepts/polis-religion.md` (fifth-century Athenian civic
religion; 1→2).

## 2026-06-11 section | CAH Vol. V (Lewis et al. 1992) | Cycle 4 — Sicily, 478–431 B.C. (Ch. 7, Asheri) | Classical Antiquity | italy, mediterranean | Pages created: 3 | Pages updated: 3

The Western Greek cycle. Asheri's chapter covers the four blocs after Himera (Carthaginian
epicracy, Syracuse/Deinomenids, Acragas/Emmenids, Rhegium/Anaxilads); the age of Hiero I
(478–467) — the battle of Cumae (474) over the Etruscans, mass deportation and the foundation
of Aitna, and the literary court (Pindar, Aeschylus, Simonides, Bacchylides); the fall of the
three tyrannies (471–462) and the autonomistic movement with its "Common Resolution"; the
Sicel movement of Ducetius (461–440) — Menainon, Morgantina, Palice, the synteleia, the
defeat at Nomae, exile and return; and the rise of democratic Syracuse (the Old/New citizen
conflict, petalismos, naval power, the new epicracy) and plutocratic Acragas (Empedocles),
with their cultural flowering (Corax/Tisias/Gorgias rhetoric, Sophron's mime, Antiochus'
history). Asheri's thesis: continuity over change. Created: `actors/hiero-i.md`,
`actors/ducetius.md`, `events/battle-of-cumae-474bce.md`. Updated: `actors/syracuse.md`
(Hiero → democracy; sources 1→2), `concepts/western-greek-tyranny.md` (the fall; 1→2),
`actors/etruscans.md` (Cumae cross-link + 453 raids; 1→2). Gorgias and Empedocles flagged for
fuller treatment in Cycle 5 (the culture chapter).

## 2026-06-11 section | CAH Vol. V (Lewis et al. 1992) | Cycle 3 — Mainland Greece 479–451 (Ch. 5, Lewis) + The Thirty Years' Peace (Ch. 6, Lewis) | Classical Antiquity | central-greece, peloponnese, aegean | Pages created: 5 | Pages updated: 2

The mainland-and-diplomacy cycle. Ch. 5 covers post-479 Sparta (the downfall of the regent
Pausanias, Leotychidas' Thessalian failure, the weak kingship), the resurgence of Argos
(destruction of Mycenae) and Arcadia (Tegea/Mantinea/Elis synoecisms; Tisamenus' five
battles), the earthquake of 464 and the Third Messenian War / Great Helot Revolt on Ithome,
Cimon's rebuff, and the First Peloponnesian War (Megarian alliance, Long Walls, fall of
Aegina, Tanagra & Oenophyta 458, the short-lived Boeotian land empire, Tolmides' periplous,
Naupactus settled with Messenians). Ch. 6 covers the Peace of Callias (c. 449, the disputed
end of the Persian war, argued from the Parthenon's financing), the instruments of empire
(cleruchies, the Coinage Decree, transferred lawcourts), the 446 crisis (Coronea, the revolts
of Euboea and Megara, Pleistoanax's invasion and withdrawal), the Thirty Years' Peace (446/5),
and "after the peace" (Thucydides son of Melesias ostracized 443, Thurii 444/3, the Samian War
440–439, Amphipolis 437/6). Created: `events/first-peloponnesian-war.md`,
`events/peace-of-callias-449bce.md`, `events/thirty-years-peace-446bce.md`,
`events/third-messenian-war-464bce.md`, `actors/pausanias-regent.md` — resolving the
`[[first-peloponnesian-war]]`, `[[peace-of-callias-449bce]]`, `[[thirty-years-peace-446bce]]`
and `[[pausanias-regent]]` dangling links. Updated: `actors/sparta.md` (Sparta after the
Persian Wars; sources 2→3), `actors/delian-league.md` (instruments of empire + the Samian
revolt). Dangling links still open: `[[parthenon]]`, `areopagus`, `[[archidamian-war]]`,
`naupactus`, `[[corinth]]` (exists?), `[[aegina]]` (exists).

## 2026-06-11 section | CAH Vol. V (Lewis et al. 1992) | Cycle 2 — The Delian League to 449 (Ch. 3, Rhodes) + The Athenian Revolution (Ch. 4, Rhodes) | Classical Antiquity | aegean, central-greece | Pages created: 7 | Pages updated: 3

The political core of the volume's first half. Ch. 3 reconstructs the Delian League from its
foundation on Delos (478/7) — organization (Delos treasury, single council, ships vs phoros,
Aristides' 460-talent assessment, the hellenotamiai) through its transformation into the
Athenian empire: the Pentekontaetia catalogue (Eion, Scyros, Carystus, Naxos "the first ally
enslaved," the Eurymedon, the Thasian war, the Egyptian disaster c. 454), the transfer of the
treasury to Athens (454/3), and the mid-century crisis read from the tribute quota-lists
(imposed democracies, episkopoi, garrisons, cleruchies, Panathenaea offerings, proprietorial
language). Ch. 4 ("the Athenian revolution") covers Ephialtes' 462/1 reform of the Areopagus,
Periclean democracy (Assembly/boule/dikasteria/sortition/pay/elected generals), Pericles'
citizenship law (451/0) and jury pay, and the long arc Draco→Solon→Cleisthenes→Ephialtes. Also
the fall of Themistocles (ostracism, medism charge, flight to Artaxerxes). Created:
`actors/delian-league.md` (major; resolves a wiki-wide dangling link), `actors/pericles.md`,
`actors/cimon.md`, `actors/ephialtes.md`, `actors/aristides-the-just.md`,
`concepts/athenian-democracy.md` (resolves the `[[athenian-democracy]]` dangling link),
`events/ephialtes-reform-462bce.md`. Updated: `actors/themistocles.md` (the fall & flight),
`concepts/ostracism.md` (fifth-century ostracisms), `places/athens-fifth-century.md` (Ephialtic
reform + empire mechanics). New dangling links to resolve later: `[[peace-of-callias-449bce]]`,
`[[thirty-years-peace-446bce]]`, `[[first-peloponnesian-war]]`, `[[pausanias-regent]]`,
`areopagus`, `[[parthenon]]`, `[[archidamian-war]]`.

## 2026-06-11 section | CAH Vol. V (Lewis et al. 1992) | Cycle 1 — Sources/Chronology/Method (Ch. 1, Lewis) + Greece after the Persian Wars (Ch. 2, Davies) | Classical Antiquity | aegean, central-greece, mediterranean | Pages created: 3 | Pages updated: 5

The framing cycle. Ch. 1 is the volume's historiographical charter: the three source-zones
(thin Pentekontaetia 478–435 via Diodorus/Ephorus; firm Thucydides 435–411; Xenophon +
Diodorus 411–404), the author roster (Thucydides, Herodotus, Diodorus/Ephorus, Hellenica
Oxyrhynchia, Xenophon, Theopompus, Plutarch, comedy/oratory, the Atthides & Ath.Pol.), the
epigraphic spine (tribute quota-lists from 453, Parthenon accounts), the Pentekontaetia
chronology problem (few fixed points; ±5–6-year uncertainties), and the Athenocentric lens
as itself a historical fact. Ch. 2 (Davies) is the structural analysis of post-479 Greece
as cultural system (the Hellene/barbaros boundary, four facets of unity), economic system
(the autarkic oikos vs the grain-trade logistics revolution; ship-type divergence; small
change, banks, bottomry loans, the Agora), and political system (the "crisis of the polis":
military unit dead, cultural/administrative unit alive; synoecism; isonomia; the
trierarchy/leitourgia fiscal system). Created: `actors/thucydides.md`,
`concepts/classical-greek-historiography.md`, `concepts/hellenic-identity.md`. Updated:
`actors/classical-greece.md` (post-Persian-War transition), `periods/classical-antiquity.md`
(fifth-century historiography + chronology; sources 1→2), `concepts/city-state.md` (the
fifth-century crisis of the polis), `concepts/coinage.md` (post-war monetization),
`processes/trade-network-formation.md` (the grain-trade revolution). Standing dangling
links to resolve in later cycles: `[[delian-league]]`, `[[peloponnesian-war]]`,
`[[ephialtes]]`, `[[pericles]]`, `[[cimon]]`, `[[brasidas]]`, `[[herodotus]]`,
`[[panhellenic-games]]`, `delos`.

## 2026-06-11 section | The Cambridge Ancient History Vol. I, Part 2: Early History of the Middle East (Edwards, Gadd & Hammond, eds., 1971) | Section 10: The Aegean EBA & the Indo-Europeans (Chs. XXVI–XXVII) | Pages created: 4 | Pages updated: 4

Created `processes/early-bronze-age-aegean.md` (the Early Helladic/Cycladic/Early
Minoan world — Melian obsidian, Cycladic marble figurines, the EH II sauceboat

technological impact, the Pontic-leaning but unidentified homeland, the
peripheral-archaism dialectology and early Anatolian split), and
`events/coming-of-the-greeks-1900bce.md` (the disputed ~1900 BCE arrival of
Greek-speakers; Minyan ware; the Mellaart/Palmer debate). Updated
`controversies/indo-european-origins.md` (a full Crossland-reassessment section,
sited between the steppe consensus and the Indo-Hittite tradition),
`periods/early-bronze-age.md` and `periods/middle-bronze-age.md` (the Aegean strand
and the Indo-European arrivals), and `places/troy.md` (Aegean trade links and the
Minyan-ware origin debate). Key content: Ch. XXVI(a) (Caskey) — Greece, Crete and
the Aegean islands in the EBA, the EH II catastrophe proven at Lerna; Ch. XXVI(b) —
Early Cypriot (Philia vs. Vounous), synchronised with the Anatolian end-EB2
catastrophe; Ch. XXVII (Crossland) — "Immigrants from the North": the
Indo-European problem, earliest attestations (Hittite-Luwian, Mitanni "Aryans",
Linear B Greek), the homeland (Gimbutas/Merpert/Thieme), and the impact of the
northerners.

## 2026-06-11 ingest-complete | CAH Vol. II, Part 1: The Middle East and the Aegean Region, c. 1800–1380 BC (Edwards, Gadd, Hammond & Sollberger, eds., 1973) | Total pages created: 35 | Total pages updated: 10 | Sections processed: 6

Completed the Large-Volume ingest of the missing Bronze-Age backbone volume (sourced
as an EPUB, converted, ingested over 6 section cycles). The volume populates the
Middle-to-Late Bronze transition across the whole eastern Mediterranean: Mesopotamia
& the Amorites/Hammurabi (S1), Egypt's SIP–Hyksos–early New Kingdom (S2), the Levant
& Mitanni (S3), Anatolia & the Hittites (S4), the Aegean — Minoan, Mycenaean & the
scripts (S5), and the Persian plateau (S6). Step-3 close-out: wrote the Volume

## 2026-06-11 section | CAH Vol. II, Part 2 (1975) | Section 5: The Iron-Age Near East — Assyria/Babylon, Elam, Syria/Philistia/Phoenicia, the Hebrew Kingdom & late-Ramesside Egypt (Ch. Section 5 carries the Near East from the Bronze Age Collapse into the early Iron Age.
Mesopotamia: the Elamite Shutrukids end Kassite Babylon; the native Second Dynasty of
Isin revives under Nebuchadnezzar I (defeat of Elam, recovery of Marduk's statue, the
god's rise to supremacy); Assyria recovers briefly under Tiglath-pileser I (the Mushki,
the Nairi, the march to the Mediterranean, the first naming of the Aramaeans) before
the Aramaean tide overwhelms both powers. Levant: the Aramaeans found their Syrian
kingdoms; Albright's Canaanite revival in Phoenicia (Tyre/Sidon, Wenamun, the Hiram
alliance, western colonization); and Eissfeldt's source-critical Hebrew Kingdom of
Saul, David and Solomon. Egypt: the late-Ramesside collapse into the High-Priests-of-
Amun / 21st-Dynasty division. Created: nebuchadnezzar-i, second-isin-dynasty,
tiglath-pileser-i, arameans, hebrew-kingdom, david, solomon. Updated: phoenicians,
assyria-middle, new-kingdom-egypt, elam, periods/early-iron-age. Albright's
idiosyncratic high chronology flagged on the Phoenician/Aramaean pages.

## 2026-06-11 section | CAH Vol. II, Part 2 (1975) | Section 6: The Greek World after the Bronze Age — the Dark Age, the migrations, Homer, Greek language & religion (Ch. XXXVI–XL) | Early Iron Age | aegean, anatolia, mediterranean-world, western-europe | Pages created: 9 | Pages updated: 5

Section 6 — the final content section — covers the Aegean and western Mediterranean
after the Mycenaean collapse. Greece passes into the Greek Dark Age (sub-Mycenaean →
Protogeometric pottery, lost Linear B literacy, palace → village). The Dorians (the
"Return of the Heracleidae") seize the Peloponnese, driving the Aeolic/Ionic/Dorian
migrations that settle the coast of Asia Minor and give rise to the polis. The oral
heroic tradition crystallizes in Homer (treated as a source for three ages — Bronze
Age, Dark Age, Ionian present — in the Homeric-Question controversy). Chadwick's
prehistory of the Greek language revises the "three waves" theory (single
Indo-European intrusion ~2100 BCE; dialects formed inside Greece; Mycenaean = East
Greek by Linear B). Guthrie's Greek religion traces the Olympian/chthonian amalgam of
Indo-European and Minoan-Mycenaean roots, with Hesiod's theogony echoing the
Hurrian-Hittite Kumarbi cycle. A western-Mediterranean chapter anchors Bronze/Iron
Age Italy (Villanovan → Etruscan), Nuraghic Sardinia (the Sherden question),
Sicily/Malta, and Iberia/Tartessus at the threshold of Phoenician and Greek
colonization. Created: `periods/greek-dark-age.md`, `actors/dorians.md`,
`events/dorian-invasion-1120bce.md`, `events/ionian-migration-1050bce.md`,
`actors/homer.md`, `controversies/homeric-poems-as-history.md`,
`concepts/coming-of-the-greeks-1900bce.md` (resolves a heavily-referenced dangling
link), `concepts/greek-religion-and-mythology.md`, `places/western-mediterranean.md`.
Updated: `actors/phrygians.md` (the European Briges), `events/trojan-war-1250bce.md`
(Homer + Mopsus/Karatepe), `actors/sea-peoples.md` (the Sherden = Sardis/Sardinia
debate), `periods/early-iron-age.md` (the Aegean developments wired in),
`processes/mycenaean-civilization.md` (its greek-dark-age forward-links now resolve).
Resolved forward-links: `[[greek-dark-age]]`, `Homer`,
`[[coming-of-the-greeks-1900bce]]`.

## 2026-06-11 section | CAH Vol. VII.2 | Section: Cycle 2 — Fifth Century Republic (Chs 4–5: Drummond on social/economic structure and the citizen community) | Pages created: 3 | Pages updated: 5

Section 2 covers the internal social, economic, and constitutional history of the
fifth-century Roman Republic. Drummond Ch. 4 analyses the social and economic
framework: subsistence emmer-wheat agriculture, endemic debt and nexum, the
patriarchal household (patria potestas), the clan (gens) as social/political but
not primary economic unit, and the patron-client system (Lapis Satricanus as
earliest epigraphic evidence). Drummond Ch. 5 analyses the citizen community: the
origins and functions of the [[roman-patriciate|patriciate]], the constitutional
structure of the early Republic (dual consulship, dictatorship, consular tribunate,
assemblies, senate), and the fifth-century phase of the [[struggle-of-the-orders]]
— creation of the tribunate (First Secession c. 494/3 BCE), the Twelve Tables
(451–449 BCE), and the limited character of fifth-century plebeian gains. The
chapter includes the volume's most detailed argument for the early authenticity of
the consular fasti and against the "patrician coup" hypothesis.

Created: `processes/struggle-of-the-orders.md` (Struggle of the Orders as a
long-duration political process, c. 494–287 BCE); `events/twelve-tables-450bce.md`
(Decemvirate and publication, causal analysis and consequences); `concepts/roman-patriciate.md`
(origins, functions, monopolies, the scholarship on closure and decline). Updated:
`actors/roman-republic.md` (added "The Early Republic: Constitutional Structure"
section — dual consulship, dictatorship, consular tribunate, patrician monopoly,
fifth-century economy); `places/rome.md` (added "The Fifth-Century City: Economy
and Social Structure" section — agriculture, debt/nexum, social stratification,
patron-client, the Aventine as plebeian space); `controversies/reliability-of-early-roman-tradition.md`
(added Drummond's Ch. 5 analysis of the consular fasti — the three lines of evidence
for authenticity, the "plebeian names" problem, and the assessment of fifth-century
narrative as largely unusable).

## 2026-06-11 section | CAH Vol. VII.2 | Section: Cycle 6 — Religion and Appendices (Ch. 12: North on Republican religion; Appendix: Drummond on early Roman chronology; Consular Fasti 509–220 BCE) | Pages created: 1 | Pages updated: 2

New pages: roman-republican-religion (concept). Updated: roman-republic (Republican Religion section added), walbank source page (counts 28→29 created, 16→18 updated; Volume Synthesis Note written). Key content: North's revisionist methodological framework — rejects all developmental narratives (Warde Fowler animism → polytheism; Latte; Dumézil tripartite ideology); treats system as broadly constant across the Republic; all sources are late Republican/Principate, filtered through Christian polemic. Four priestly colleges: pontifices (widest remit; calendar; family law), augures (auspices; templum; pomerium), fetiales (just war protocol; treaties), decemviri sacris faciundis (Sibylline Books; prodigy remedia). Priests are NOT a separate caste — same nobles who dominate politics. Religious authority diffused across senate + magistrates + colleges; no monopoly possible. Gods as contractual partners (vota are binding formulae); prodigies as disrupted divine-human relationship requiring remedia; sacrifice (*litatio* required). Vestals as the most liminal figures: priests, virgins, fire-keepers, guardians of pignora imperii; pontifex maximus holds special authority over them. Transition from monarchy: rex sacrorum (title preserved, politically castrated; excluded from Senate and all office); Jupiter Capitolinus as continuity with Etruscan-regal ceremonial (triumph = general dressed as Jupiter/king — not rejected but embraced by the Republic). Innovation as normal functioning: Aesculapius 290s, Victoria temple 294 BCE, Greek rites of Ceres (third century with Greek priestesses granted citizenship), lectisternium 399 BCE. Sibylline Books as legitimation mechanism for new cults. Patrician religious monopoly via college co-optation; broken by Lex Ogulnia 300 BCE (plebeians admitted to pontificate and augurate); pontifex maximus election reform probably before Ti. Coruncanius (first plebeian holder, c. 253 BCE). Bacchanalian affair 186 BCE as first sign of new religious age (autonomous religious groups — alien to the entire system). Drummond Appendix: Varronian chronology is a mid-first-century BCE creation (probably Atticus not Varro); incorporates fictitious "dictator-years"; all dates before c. 300 BCE are reconstructed from consular lists that ancient historians already knew were defective. Consular Fasti 509–220 BCE: reference list of consuls year by year, heavily annotated with variants; purely reference material, no new analytical pages created.

## 2026-06-12 section | NCMH Vol. 1 | Section: Cycle 1 — Foundations & Sources (Intro, Chs 1–3) | Pages created: 1 | Pages updated: 5

Section 1 (Cycle 1) covers the introduction and Chapters 1 to 3 of the volume. Chapter 1 (Gerberding) details the late Roman fiscal-military machine, Diocletian's and Constantine's administrative and tax reforms (annona, caput, tetrarchy), the decline of municipal self-government (curiae) to centralized bureaucracy, and the institutionalization of early Christianity (Nicaea 325, Arianism). Chapter 2 (Halsall) provides a critical analysis of the "Barbarian Invasions," refuting both the catastrophist "Germanist" (mass migrations) and minimalist "Romanist" (simple elite accommodation) models. Halsall argues that the collapse of the Roman state created local political and administrative vacuums which drew in barbarian groups (migrations as result, not cause), leading to fluid ethnic constructions. Chapter 3 (Halsall) surveys sources and interpretation, addressing charters (formulaic arengae, dispositive clauses, witness lists, regnal years vs. AD dating) and letters (the Austrasian Letters), hagiography and verse panegyrics, and the evolution of early medieval archaeology. It tracks the theoretical shift from Culture History (equating archaeological cultures with biological/genetic peoples) to processualism and post-processualism, which treat grave-goods and furnished burial rites not as passive indicators of pagan religion or race, but as active symbolic communication and strategies of ethnogenesis during times of local social competition and instability. Also covers epigraphic and numismatic evidence, highlighting Viennese/Lyonnaise consular calendars and the Spanish Era as indicators of regional identity.

Created: `wiki/concepts/ethnogenesis.md`. Updated: `wiki/periods/late-antiquity.md`, `wiki/periods/early-middle-ages.md`, `wiki/controversies/causes-of-the-fall-of-the-western-roman-empire.md`, `wiki/controversies/fall-of-rome-causes.md`, `wiki/actors/germanic-peoples.md`, and the volume's own source page.

## 2026-06-12 section | NCMH Vol. 1 | Section: Cycle 2 — The Sixth Century (Part I, Chs 4–10) | Pages created: 9 | Pages updated: 5

Section 2 (Cycle 2) covers Chapters 4 to 10 of the volume. Chapter 4 (Louth) details the reigns of Anastasius, Justin I, Justinian, Justin II, Tiberius II, and Maurice, highlighting Justinian's domestic programs (Corpus Iuris Civilis, John the Cappadocian's fiscal extraction, the Nika Riots of 532, and Hagia Sophia's reconstruction) and the severe demographic and fiscal-military impact of the Justinianic Plague (541–542), as well as his religious policy (Three Chapters Controversy, Fifth Ecumenical Council in 553) and Maurice's exarchates (Ravenna and Carthage). Chapter 5 (Moorhead) details the Byzantine reconquests of North Africa (Vandalic War) and Italy (Gothic War, Belisarius's early campaigns, Totila's social-peasant resistance, and Narses's victory and the Pragmatic Sanction of 554), arguing that the Gothic War devastated Italy, destroyed the Ostrogothic bridge, and accelerated the East-West division. Chapter 6 (Moorhead) details Ostrogothic dualism (Cassiodorus, Boethius) and the Lombard Migration (568) under Alboin, which fragmented Italy permanently, collapsed the Roman senatorial aristocracy, and enabled the rise of the papacy under Gregory the Great. Chapter 7 (Barbero/Loring) traces the Suevic kingdom in Gallaecia, Leovigild's unification of Visigothic Spain and annexation of the Sueves (585) after crushing Hermenegild's rebellion, and Reccared's conversion to Catholicism at the Third Council of Toledo (589), uniting the Goths and Hispano-Romans under a territorial law code (eventual Liber Iudiciorum) and starting anti-Jewish canons. Chapter 8 (Van Dam) details Clovis's conversion, Merovingian succession partitions, the civic administration of bishops, and Gregory of Tours's theological framing in his Histories. Chapter 9 (Davies) outlines Celtic kingship, clientship, and monastic paruchiae (Columba at Iona, Columbanus). Chapter 10 (Hamerow) reassesses the Anglo-Saxon migrations through settlement and cemetery archaeology, refuting mass replacement models in favor of cultural assimilation, and outlining kingdom formation through competitive exclusion.

Created: `wiki/events/nika-riots-532.md`, `wiki/events/justinianic-plague-541.md`, `wiki/events/gothic-war-535-554.md`, `wiki/events/lombard-invasion-568.md`, `wiki/events/third-council-of-toledo-589.md`, `wiki/actors/belisarius.md`, `wiki/actors/leovigild.md`, `wiki/actors/gregory-of-tours.md`, and `wiki/controversies/three-chapters-controversy.md`. Updated: `wiki/actors/justinian.md`, `wiki/actors/successors-of-justinian.md`, `wiki/actors/lombards.md`, `wiki/actors/vandal-kingdom.md`, `wiki/processes/post-roman-transformation-of-the-west.md`, and the volume's own source page.

## 2026-06-12 section | NCMH Vol. 1 | Section: Cycle 3 — The Seventh Century (Part II, Chs 11–19) | Pages created: 18 | Pages updated: 2

Section 3 (Cycle 3) covers Chapters 11 to 19 of the volume. Chapter 11 (Louth) details the reigns of Phocas and Heraclius, the final Sasanian War (the Persian conquest of Egypt/Syria, the Siege of Constantinople in 626, and Heraclius's counter-offensive culminating in the Battle of Nineveh in 627), the Arab conquests (loss of Syria at Yarmouk in 636 and Egypt in 642), the failed Monothelite theological compromise and the Sixth Ecumenical Council (680–681), and the emergence of the theme system under Heraclius's successors (Constans II, Constantine IV, Justinian II). Chapter 12 (Hillenbrand) details the pre-Islamic context, Muhammad's career (the Hijra in 622), the Rashidun Caliphate and early conquests, the First Fitna civil war, the rise of the Umayyad dynasty in Damascus under Mu'awiya I, the Second Fitna, and Caliph Abd al-Malik's consolidation and reforms (Arabization of the bureaucracy, Islamic coinage, and the construction of the Dome of the Rock). Chapter 13 (Claude) details Visigothic Catholic kingship, intellectual culture (Isidore of Seville), anti-Jewish legislation, and the legal unification of Romans and Goths under the Liber Iudiciorum in 654. Chapter 14 (Fouracre) details Merovingian decentralization, the rise of the Pippinid/Arnulfing Mayors of the Palace in Austrasia, and Pippin II of Herstal's victory at the Battle of Tertry (687), establishing Pippinid hegemony. Chapters 15 and 16 (Ó Cróinín, Stancliffe) outline early Irish and British monastic developments and missionary movements (peregrinatio pro Christo). Chapter 17 (Thacker) details the Christianisation of Anglo-Saxon England, the clash of Roman and Irish missions culminating in the Synod of Whitby (664), the reforms of Theodore of Tarsus, and the Battle of Nechtansmere (685) which ended Northumbrian overlordship in the north. Chapter 18 (Hedeager) outlines the Scandinavian Vendel Period (c. 550–800) characterized by elite boat graves (Vendel, Valsgärde, Sutton Hoo ties), long-distance trade, and zoomorphic art. Chapter 19 (Kobylinski) traces Slavic migrations, demographic growth, Avar relations, and the tribal federation of Samo's Empire (r. 623–658).

## 2026-06-12 section | NCMH Vol. 1 | Section: Cycle 4 — Themes and Problems (Part III, Chs 20–28B) | Pages created: 8 | Pages updated: 2

Section 4 (Cycle 4) covers Chapters 20 to 28B of the volume. Chapter 20 (Toch) traces the legal standing of Jews in the early medieval successor kingdoms, analyzing the Roman framework of *religio licita* and its progressive dismantling: Ostrogothic Italy maintained Roman privilege; Merovingian Gaul broadly preserved toleration in practice despite conciliar legislation; Visigothic Spain destroyed it systematically — Sisebut's forced baptisms (616 CE), the Liber Iudiciorum (654), and the 17th Council of Toledo (694) under Egica reducing Jews to state slavery. Chapter 21 (Wormald) surveys the *leges nationum* — Salic, Ripuarian, Lombard, Visigothic, and Anglo-Saxon codes — and their central wergild structures (including the explicit ethnic hierarchy in Salic law, with Romans at 100 *solidi* vs. Franks at 200). Wormald argues these codes were ideological acts of royal legitimacy imitating Roman legal prestige, not practical administrative manuals; the ideology of kingship was simultaneously shifting to a biblical (Old Testament) model after the Visigothic conversion. Chapter 22 (Loseby) and Chapter 23 (Blackburn/Lebecq) address the economic transformation: the contraction of the Mediterranean trading network and the rise of the North Sea commercial zone anchored on the wics/emporia (Hamwic, Lundenwic, Ipswich, Quentovic, Dorestad), Frisian merchants, and the small silver sceatta coinage that replaced the Merovingian gold tremissis from the 670s–680s CE. Chapter 24 (Scheibelreiter) analyzes the reality of early medieval church structure: the theoretical episcopal hierarchy was overridden by the Eigenkirche (proprietary church) system, and the royal synod replaced the canonical council as the effective instrument of church governance. Chapter 25 (Wood) covers Christianisation: top-down royal conversion, the cult of saints and relics as conversion mechanisms, and the syncretist persistence of pre-Christian practices. Chapters 26 (Fontaine) covers education and learning: Cassiodorus's Vivarium, Isidore's Etymologiae, and the Northumbrian school (Aldhelm, Bede). Chapters 27–28B (Brubaker et al.) survey early medieval art and architecture: Insular art as a synthesis of Celtic, Germanic, and Mediterranean traditions (Lindisfarne Gospels, Book of Kells, Sutton Hoo); the contrast with Byzantine domed architecture (Hagia Sophia, San Vitale); Visigothic metalwork (Guarrazar Treasure); and the Carolingian classical revival that displaced Insular principles after c. 800 CE.

Created: `wiki/concepts/religio-licita.md`, `wiki/concepts/wergild.md`, `wiki/concepts/wic.md`, `wiki/concepts/sceatta.md`, `wiki/concepts/eigenkirche.md`, `wiki/concepts/insular-art.md`, `wiki/processes/christianisation-of-anglo-saxon-england.md`, and `wiki/controversies/pirenne-thesis.md`. Updated: `wiki/periods/early-middle-ages.md` and `wiki/processes/post-roman-transformation-of-the-west.md`.

## 2026-06-12 section | NCMH Vol. 3 — c. 900–c. 1024 (Reuter ed., 1999) | Section: Cycle 1 — Reading the Tenth Century & Common Themes (Intro + Part I, Chs 1–7) | Pages created: 6 | Pages updated: 4

Began the Large-Volume ingest of NCMH III (the "long tenth century," c. 900–c. 1024). Step 1 (structural map + 5-cycle Section Plan) written to `sources/reuter-ncmh-v3-1999.md`. Cycle 1 covers Reuter's historiographical Introduction ("reading the tenth century") and Part I's seven thematic chapters: Fossier on rural economy (the "take-off" of the European economy; the lineage→house family shift; the gradations of freedom from allod to serfdom; the trans-European slave trade and the decline of true slavery after the Christianisation of the Slavs/Magyars); Johanek on merchants/markets/towns (the origins of the medieval town and the commercial revolution in the tenth century; three monetary zones; Harz silver; Córdoba and the Islamic cities as the era's true urban centres); Nelson on rulers and government (Christ-centred kingship; the detachment of Carolingian traditions from the Carolingian dynasty onto Ottonians/Capetians/the new peripheral kingdoms; the aristocracy's investment in the "regnal community" as the key to realm-survival; the drift toward indivisibility; the Ottonian itinerant kingship and dynastic convents); McKitterick on the Church (the bishop as the key tenth-century figure, the muted papacy); Wollasch on monasticism (the "first wave of reform" as a multi-centred movement — Cluny's unique self-directed *libertas* vs. proprietary Gorze/Brogne; the *ordo Cluniacensis* as a way of life not an order; the eremitic strand — Romuald); Leonardi on intellectual life (against the "stagnation" thesis; the broadened classical canon; Gerbert of Aurillac and Abbo of Fleury; the new self-conscious individual intellectual); Mayr-Harting on art and patronage (the Ottonian Renaissance). The volume's organising thread — the **"feudal revolution"/*mutation de l'an mil*** debate, which Reuter pointedly declines to synthesise — is captured as a standing controversy.

Created: `controversies/feudal-revolution-debate.md`, `concepts/encellulement.md`, `actors/cluny.md`, `actors/gerbert-of-aurillac.md`, `concepts/peace-of-god.md`, `concepts/ottonian-renaissance.md`. Updated: `concepts/bipartite-manor.md` (the tenth-century dissolution of the *Villikation* and the contested slavery→serfdom shift), `processes/urbanization.md` (added the medieval European urban-revival phase), `periods/early-middle-ages.md` (long-tenth-century post-Carolingian section; sources 5→6), `periods/high-middle-ages.md` (NCMH III coverage of the period's opening; the *mutation* debate as periodisation hinge; sources 1→2).

## 2026-06-12 section | NCMH Vol. 3 — c. 900–c. 1024 (Reuter ed., 1999) | Section: Cycle 2 — The Ottonian/German World (Part II-A, Chs 8–12) | Pages created: 8 | Pages updated: 1

Cycle 2 covers the post-Carolingian core: Müller-Mertens's keystone chapter on the Ottonians as kings and emperors, plus the regional chapters on Saxony and the Elbe Slavs, Bavaria, Lotharingia, and Burgundy/Provence. The narrative runs from the failure of the late Carolingian and Conradine east-Frankish kingship, through Henry I's consensual, friendship-pact kingship and his declaration of indivisibility, to Otto I's assertive sacral kingship — the family revolts of 936–954, the annihilation of the Magyars at the Lechfeld (955), the conquest of Italy and the imperial coronation of 962, the foundation of Magdeburg, and the building of the imperial church. It continues through Otto II's disasters (Cotrone 982, the Great Slav Revolt of 983), the regencies of Theophanu and Adelaide, Otto III's Renovatio Imperii Romanorum (the Palatine palace, Gerbert/Sylvester II, the Gniezno pilgrimage of 1000, the new Christian kingships of Poland and Hungary), and Henry II's reversal toward a German-focused renovatio regni Francorum (Bamberg 1007, the Liutizi alliance against Poland, the Reichskirche at its apogee, the regnum Teutonicum). Müller-Mertens's analytical frame of itinerant kingship — central regions (Harz/Saxony, Rhine–Main, Aachen), transit regions, and distant zones — is captured on the dynasty page.

Created: `actors/ottonian-dynasty.md`, `actors/otto-i.md`, `actors/otto-iii.md`, `actors/henry-ii-holy-roman-emperor.md`, `events/battle-of-lechfeld-955.md`, `events/coronation-of-otto-i-962.md`, `concepts/reichskirche.md`, `actors/elbe-slavs.md`. Updated: `periods/early-middle-ages.md` (Ottonian dynasty/Lechfeld/962 added to Key Actors). Forward-links left to resolve in later cycles: [[kingdom-of-italy]], [[capetian-dynasty]] (Cycle 3); [[synod-of-gniezno-1000]], [[boleslaw-i-chrobry]], [[hungary-arpad]], [[stephen-i-of-hungary]] (Cycle 4).

## 2026-06-12 section | NCMH Vol. 3 — c. 900–c. 1024 (Reuter ed., 1999) | Section: Cycle 3 — Italy, West Francia & England (Part II-B, Chs 13–17) | Pages created: 4 | Pages updated: 1

Cycle 3 covers the rest of the post-Carolingian core: Sergi on the kingdom of Italy, Dunbabin on the west Frankish kingdom and its principalities, and Keynes on England. Italy: the chaotic "national" kings (Berengar I, Wido/Lambert of Spoleto, Hugh of Arles, Berengar II), the bloodbath of Fiorenzuola (923), and the Ottonian conquest of 961–962; Sergi's reading of Carolingian forms with fragmenting substance (the arimanni as descendants of the free liberi homines, the devolution of immunity/districtus — Italian incastellamento). West Francia: the failing last Carolingians (Charles the Simple to Louis V), the Robertine "wall," the Treaty of Saint-Clair-sur-Epte (911) founding Normandy, the Capetian accession of 987 (Adalbero of Rheims, the rejection of Charles of Lorraine, associative succession), and the territorial principalities as the classic ground of the mutation de l'an mil. England: Keynes's account of the West Saxon unification (Alfred → Edward the Elder & Æthelflæd → Æthelstan/Brunanburh 937 → Edgar/Bath 973), the unusually strong late Anglo-Saxon administrative state (shire/hundred/burh/coinage/writ), the English Benedictine reform (Dunstan, Æthelwold, Oswald; Regularis Concordia), and the collapse under Æthelred II into the Danish conquest of 1016 — with the explicit note that the feudal-revolution model is rejected for England.

Created: `actors/kingdom-of-italy.md`, `actors/capetian-dynasty.md`, `actors/kingdom-of-england-late-anglo-saxon.md`, `events/treaty-of-saint-clair-sur-epte-911.md`. Updated: `periods/early-middle-ages.md` (post-Carolingian western kingdoms added to Key Actors). Forward-link `[[southern-italy-lombard-principalities]]` left for Cycle 5.

## 2026-06-12 section | NCMH Vol. 3 — c. 900–c. 1024 (Reuter ed., 1999) | Section: Cycle 4 — The Emergent East (Part III-A, Chs 18–20) | Pages created: 7 | Pages updated: 1

Cycle 4 covers the Christian state-formation of the eastern and northern periphery: Noonan on European Russia, Strzelczyk on Bohemia and Poland, Bakay on Hungary. Russia: the wider world of European Russia (Cherson, the Khazar khaganate, the Volga Bulgars, the steppe Pechenegs, the east Slavs), the source problem (the post-1050 Primary Chronicle), the Rurikid Rus′ state on the Varangian river routes, and the watershed conversion of Vladimir I in 988 (the Cherson campaign, the Anna marriage, the turn to Constantinople not Rome). Poland and Bohemia as "two successful western Slavonic state-formations": the Piast foundation (Mieszko I's baptism 966; Bolesław Chrobry's expansion, Gniezno 1000, the wars with Henry II, the Peace of Bautzen 1018, the royal coronation 1025) and Přemyslid Bohemia (Wenceslas, the Slavnikid massacre at Libice 995, St Adalbert, Prague as a Mainz-dependent bishopric, the Polish occupation of 1003, Břetislav I's reconstruction and Bohemia as an imperial fief). Hungary: the Magyar conquest of the Carpathian basin, the age of raids broken at Riade (933) and the Lechfeld (955), and the Christian kingdom of St Stephen (crowned c. 1000/1001, Esztergom, the county system). The Act of Gniezno and the Hungarian church are tied to Otto III's Renovatio. This cycle resolved all the forward-links left dangling from Cycles 2–3 ([[synod-of-gniezno-1000]], [[boleslaw-i-chrobry]], [[hungary-arpad]], [[stephen-i-of-hungary]]).

Created: `actors/kievan-rus.md`, `events/conversion-of-vladimir-988.md`, `actors/boleslaw-i-chrobry.md`, `actors/premyslid-bohemia.md`, `events/synod-of-gniezno-1000.md`, `actors/hungary-arpad.md`, `actors/stephen-i-of-hungary.md`. Updated: `periods/early-middle-ages.md` (millennial Christian state-formation of the eastern periphery added to Slav Expansions).

## 2026-06-12 section | NCMH Vol. 3 — c. 900–c. 1024 (Reuter ed., 1999) | Section: Cycle 5 — Byzantium & the Mediterranean (Part III-B, Chs 21–27) | Pages created: 6 | Pages updated: 3

Cycle 5, the final content cycle, covers the court-centred, urbanised, taxing/legislating polities for which Reuter notes the western periodisations do not fit. Shepard's two Byzantium chapters trace the Macedonian dynasty from defensive equilibrium (Leo VI's law-books and Book of the Eparch; Constantine VII's scholar-emperorship and "Solomonic" palace rule; the Arab sea-raids) to expansion (Nikephoros Phokas's reconquest of Crete 961 and Antioch 969, John Tzimiskes, and Basil II), with the recurring tension between the central state and the dynatoi military aristocracy. Basil II "the Bulgar-Slayer": the aristocratic revolts (Skleros, Phokas), the Rus′/Varangian alliance and the conversion of 988, and the destruction of Samuel's Bulgaria (Kleidion 1014, annexation 1018) restoring the Danube frontier — the empire's medieval apogee. Kennedy on al-Andalus: the Caliphate of Córdoba (Abd al-Rahman III/al-Nasir's reunification, the caliphal title 929, the slave- and Berber-soldier army, Madinat al-Zahra; al-Hakam II; al-Mansur/Almanzor's dictatorship and raids on Barcelona 985 and Santiago 997; the fitna and 1031 collapse). Collins on the Christian Spanish kingdoms (León as heir to Asturias, the repoblación of the Duero, the county of Castile, Sancho III of Navarre, the Catalan counties) and southern Italy (the Lombard principalities, the Byzantine katepanate of Bari, Arab Sicily, and the maritime duchies — the fragmentation that prefigured the Norman conquest). This cycle resolved the forward-link [[southern-italy-lombard-principalities]] left from Cycle 3.

Created: `actors/macedonian-dynasty.md`, `actors/basil-ii.md`, `events/conquest-of-bulgaria-1018.md`, `actors/caliphate-of-cordoba.md`, `actors/christian-kingdoms-of-spain.md`, `actors/southern-italy-lombard-principalities.md`. Updated: `actors/byzantine-empire.md` (the Macedonian apogee 886–1025; sources 3→4), `actors/first-bulgarian-empire.md` (Symeon's apogee and Basil II's conquest 1018; sources 1→2), `actors/kingdom-of-asturias.md` (its transformation into the kingdom of León).

## 2026-06-12 ingest-complete | NCMH Vol. 3 — c. 900–c. 1024 (Reuter ed., 1999) | Total pages created: 31 | Total pages updated: 7 | Sections processed: 5

All five section cycles fully processed and the Step 3 cross-section synthesis pass complete; the Volume Synthesis Note is written in `sources/reuter-ncmh-v3-1999.md`. The volume is the wiki's "long tenth century" backbone (post-Carolingian Europe, c. 900–c. 1024), built around Reuter's reasoned refusal to synthesise the **feudal-revolution/*mutation de l'an mil*** debate. Whole-volume connections drawn in synthesis: (1) the **detachment of Carolingian ideology from the Carolingian dynasty** onto Ottonians, Capetians, and the West Saxon kings (Nelson's "regnal community" and the drift to indivisible succession); (2) the **strong-vs-fragmented monarchy** contrast (Ottonian Reich/England vs. west Francia/Italy); (3) the **Otto III–Sylvester II Renovatio nexus** linking Cycles 2 and 4 (Gniezno 1000, St Stephen's crown, the Christianisation of Poland/Bohemia/Hungary; Rus′ taking Orthodoxy in 988); and (4) the **inapplicability of western periodisations** to court-centred Byzantium and Córdoba (Cycle 5). Cross-volume tension flagged: NCMH III inverts NCMH II (consolidation → devolution) while stressing ideological continuity, and its anti-*mutation* scepticism parallels the wiki's transformation-vs-catastrophe framing of the fall of Rome. Step 4: the source is already in converted-markdown form in `raw/Markdown/` (no PDF to convert/delete), consistent with the filing of NCMH I and II; left in place (the `(1)` duplicate copy is a confirmed Processing-List duplicate, left untouched). index.md statistics refreshed (total 865→897; the High-Middle-Ages period upgraded to two ingested sources). Next backbone volume in Processing-List order: NCMH IV (c. 1024–c. 1198), in two parts. Git commit and push follow.

## 2026-06-12 section | NCMH Vol. 4 — c. 1024–c. 1198 (Luscombe & Riley-Smith eds., 2004) | Section: Cycle 9 — The Angevin/Capetian west, the Latin East & Islam (Part 2, Chs 17–23; completes the volume) | Pages created: 9 | Pages updated: 0

Cycle 9, the final content cycle, covers the climax of the twelfth-century western and eastern narratives. Baldwin/Bur (Ch. 17) on the resurgent Capetian monarchy from Louis VI to Philip Augustus. Keefe (Ch. 18) on Henry II and the Angevin empire — the assembly of the great cross-Channel dominion, the common law, the Becket conflict, and the empire's collapse under John (Normandy lost 1204). Barrow (Ch. 19) on Scotland/Wales/Ireland (folded). Magdalino (Ch. 20) on Byzantium 1118–1204, ending at the Fourth Crusade. Mayer (Ch. 21) on the Latin east — the four crusader states, the Muslim counter-offensive (Zengi, Nur al-Din), and the catastrophe of Hattin (1187). Brett (Ch. 22) on the Abbasids, Fatimids, and Seljuqs; Humphreys (Ch. 23) on the Zengids, Ayyubids, and the rise of Saladin, who united Egypt and Syria, won Hattin, and retook Jerusalem.

## 2026-06-12 section | NCMH Vol. 4 — c. 1024–c. 1198 (Luscombe & Riley-Smith eds., 2004) | Section: Cycle 8 — The 12th-century West: papacy, empire, Italy, Spain (Part 2, Chs 13–16) | Pages created: 5 | Pages updated: 1

Cycle 8 covers the twelfth-century western political narrative. Robinson (Ch. 13, the papacy 1122–1198) on the post-Worms papacy — Alexander III, the long schism with Barbarossa, and the Third Lateran Council (1179). Arnold (Ch. 14, the western empire 1125–1197) on the Welf–Staufen feud, Lothar III, and above all Frederick Barbarossa's attempt to restore imperial authority through a revived Roman-law imperium against the communes and the papacy (Roncaglia 1158, the destruction of Milan 1162, the papal schism, the defeat at Legnano 1176, the Peaces of Venice 1177 and Constance 1183), culminating in the dynastic union of the empire with Sicily through Henry VI and Constance. Tabacco (Ch. 15a) on the north-Italian communes; Loud (Ch. 15b) on Norman Sicily — Roger II's unification and coronation (1130), the wealthy, centralised, multicultural regno. Linehan (Ch. 16) on twelfth-century Spain (the Almoravid/Almohad period and the rising peninsular kingdoms, folded into the Cycle-4 Iberian pages).

## 2026-06-12 section | NCMH Vol. 4 — c. 1024–c. 1198 (Luscombe & Riley-Smith eds., 2004) | Section: Cycle 7 — Byzantium & the eastern/northern realms (Part 2, Chs 8–12) | Pages created: 3 | Pages updated: 3

Cycle 7 covers Byzantium and the eastern/northern periphery. Angold (Ch. 8, the Byzantine empire 1025–1118) on the astonishing reversal — from Basil II's dominance to near-collapse within fifty years (Manzikert 1071 and the ensuing civil wars losing Anatolia to the Seljuks, the Danube to the Pechenegs, Italy to the Normans), then the Komnenian restoration under Alexios I (who solicited and then channelled the First Crusade). Dimnik (Ch. 9) on the post-Yaroslav fragmentation of Kievan Rus′ into competing principalities. Wyrozumski (Ch. 10, Poland) and Sawyer (Ch. 11, Scandinavia) on the consolidation and Christianisation of the northern kingdoms (folded into existing pages). Berend (Ch. 12, Hungary) on the consolidated Árpád kingdom of Ladislas I and Coloman, the acquisition of Croatia (1102), and the twelfth-century Byzantine frontier (Manuel I; Béla III).

Created: `events/battle-of-manzikert-1071.md`, `processes/komnenian-restoration.md`, `actors/alexios-i-komnenos.md`. ## 2026-06-12 section | NCMH Vol. 4 — c. 1024–c. 1198 (Luscombe & Riley-Smith eds., 2004) | Section: Cycle 6 — Western kingdoms in the 11th c.: Franks, Spain, England (Part 2, Chs 5–7) | Pages created: 4 | Pages updated: 2

Cycle 6 covers the eleventh-century political narrative of the three western monarchies. Bouchard (Ch. 5, the kingdom of the Franks to 1108) on the early Capetians (Robert II, Henry I, Philip I) — politically weak, their domain shrunk to the Île-de-France, yet dynastically secure and never seriously challenged for the throne (the low ebb before the twelfth-century recovery). Barton (Ch. 6, Spain in the eleventh century) on the Christian ascendancy: Sancho III the Great of Navarre and the division of his realms (the birth of Castile, Aragón, Navarre as kingdoms), Fernando I's reunion of Castile-León, the parias-tribute system, Alfonso VI's capture of Toledo (1085), and El Cid's Valencia (1094). Chibnall (Ch. 7, England and Normandy 1042–1137) on "the century of the Norman Conquest": Edward the Confessor, the disputed succession, Hastings (1066), William the Conqueror's union of Normandy and England, the replacement of the Anglo-Saxon aristocracy atop a precociously strong administrative state, and Domesday Book (1086).

Created: `events/norman-conquest-of-england-1066.md`, `actors/william-the-conqueror.md`, `actors/el-cid.md`, `events/domesday-book.md`. ## 2026-06-12 section | NCMH Vol. 4 — c. 1024–c. 1198 (Luscombe & Riley-Smith eds., 2004) | Section: Cycle 5 — Reform papacy, Salian empire, Italy in the 11th c. (Part 2, Chs 2–4) | Pages created: 4 | Pages updated: 0

Began Part 2 (the regional/political narrative, complementary to Part 1's themes). Cycle 5 supplies the political spine of the reform crisis. Blumenthal (Ch. 2, the papacy 1024–1122) on the reform papacy's emancipation — the imperial reform under Henry III (Sutri 1046, Leo IX), the Election Decree of 1059 vesting election in the cardinals, the Norman alliance, and the Gregorian confrontation. Vollrath (Ch. 3, the western empire under the Salians) on the Salian century in two halves — strong customary kingship under Conrad II and Henry III, then the "crisis of medieval Germany" under Henry IV (the Gregorian Reform combining with the Saxon revolt) and Henry V, ending at the Concordat of Worms (1122) with German kingship structurally weakened and the princes empowered. Tabacco (Ch. 4a, northern/central Italy) on the decline of local public power and the church of Rome organising regional rule. Loud (Ch. 4b, southern Italy) on the three-zone Mezzogiorno (Byzantine Apulia/Calabria, Arab Sicily, the Lombard principalities) and the Norman conquest — the Hautevilles, Robert Guiscard, the papal investiture at Melfi (1059), the fall of Bari (1071), and Roger I's conquest of Sicily (1061–91).

Created: `actors/salian-dynasty.md`, `actors/henry-iv-holy-roman-emperor.md`, `events/norman-conquest-of-southern-italy.md`, `actors/papacy.md`. ## 2026-06-12 section | NCMH Vol. 4 — c. 1024–c. 1198 (Luscombe & Riley-Smith eds., 2004) | Section: Cycle 4 — al-Andalus, the Jews, literature, art (Part 1, Chs 16–19; completes Part 1) | Pages created: 8 | Pages updated: 1

Cycle 4 completes the thematic Part 1 with its frontier-religious and cultural chapters. Kennedy (Ch. 16, Muslim Spain) on the collapse of the Caliphate of Córdoba (in 1000 "almost certainly the richest and most powerful polity in western Europe") into the demilitarised taifa kingdoms, the parias-tribute system, the fall of Toledo to Alfonso VI (1085), and the successive North African Berber interventions — the Almoravids (after Sagrajas, 1086) and Almohads (after 1147) — that halted and then lost the Reconquista (Las Navas de Tolosa, 1212). Chazan (Ch. 17, the Jews) on Sephardic Jewry's Golden Age under Islam (disrupted by the Almohads) and the new Ashkenazi communities of northern Europe — immigrant traders allied with rulers, shifting to moneylending, and facing an unusually high anti-Judaism that erupted in the Rhineland massacres of 1096. Ziolkowski (Ch. 18) on the Latin–vernacular "paradox" (a Latin golden age beside the rise of the chanson de geste, troubadour lyric, and Chrétien's romance). Kidson (Ch. 19) on the Romanesque zenith and the birth of Gothic at Suger's Saint-Denis (c. 1140).

Created: `processes/reconquista.md`, `actors/almoravids.md`, `actors/almohads.md`, `places/al-andalus.md`, `actors/ashkenazi-jewry.md`, `concepts/romanesque.md`, `concepts/gothic-architecture.md`, `concepts/chanson-de-geste.md`. Updated: `actors/caliphate-of-cordoba.md` (sources 1→2). Resolved the period page's dangling [[reconquista]] link. Part 1 (the thematic half of NCMH IV) is now fully ingested across Cycles 1–4; Part 2 (the regional narrative) follows in Cycles 5–9.

## 2026-06-12 section | NCMH Vol. 4 — c. 1024–c. 1198 (Luscombe & Riley-Smith eds., 2004) | Section: Cycle 3 — Learning, the laity, crusading, the eastern churches (Part 1, Chs 12–15) | Pages created: 6 | Pages updated: 0

Cycle 3 covers Part 1's intellectual, devotional, and frontier-religious chapters. Luscombe (Ch. 12, thought and learning) on the twelfth-century renaissance: the shift from monastery to cathedral/urban school to university, lay as well as clerical literacy, scholasticism (Abelard, John of Salisbury), and the translation movement bringing Aristotle and Greco-Arabic science into Latin Europe (Adelard of Bath; Averroes). Hamilton (Ch. 13, religion and the laity) on lay piety, pilgrimage, the cult of saints, and the rise of heresy (Cathars, Waldensians, Peter Valdès). Riley-Smith (Ch. 14, the crusades 1095–1198): the definitive treatment — the First Crusade proclaimed by Urban II at Clermont (1095) as a penitential war of liberation (of eastern Christians and of Jerusalem), defined by vow, cross, and indulgence; rooted so deeply in the eleventh-century reformation and just-war theology (Anselm of Lucca, Gratian C.23) that the Turkish invasions were occasion more than cause; the military orders (Templars, Hospitallers); the Second Crusade (after the fall of Edessa 1144, preached by Bernard). Richard (Ch. 15, the eastern churches) on the Chalcedonian Orthodox, Miaphysite (Syriac, Armenian, Coptic), Church of the East, and Maronite communions, the 1054 schism, and the Latin pressure for union — part of the volume's insistence that Christendom and Europe are not coterminous.

Created: `processes/crusades.md`, `events/first-crusade-1095.md`, `actors/urban-ii.md`, `concepts/twelfth-century-renaissance.md`, `concepts/medieval-university.md`, `concepts/eastern-churches.md`. These resolved the High-Middle-Ages period page's previously dangling [[crusades]] and [[medieval-university]] links and the Cycle-2 [[urban-ii]] forward link. A dedicated heresy/Cathar page is deferred to NCMH V (the Albigensian Crusade).

## 2026-06-12 section | NCMH Vol. 4 — c. 1024–c. 1198 (Luscombe & Riley-Smith eds., 2004) | Section: Cycle 2 — Church reform: the Gregorian Reform & Investiture Contest (Part 1, Chs 8–11) | Pages created: 7 | Pages updated: 3

Cycle 2 covers Part 1's ecclesiastical-thematic core. Cowdrey (Ch. 8, structure of the church 1024–1073) on the as-yet-unstructured imperial church and its reform from above — Henry III's Synod of Sutri (1046) setting aside three rival popes, and Leo IX's reforming councils ("the apostolic see has returned from darkness to light"). Robinson (Ch. 9, Reform and the church 1073–1122) on the Gregorian Reform understood by contemporaries as a backward-looking *renovatio* to an apostolic golden age, purging the "diseases" of simony, clerical marriage, and lay investiture in the name of the *libertas ecclesiae* — and on the Investiture Contest fought as much in polemic (the Wibertine/Henrician vs. Gregorian parties, each claiming the apostolic past) as in arms. Constable (Ch. 10, religious communities 1024–1215) on the greatest expansion and diversification of the religious life in European history — Cluny's apogee, the eremitical Carthusians, the Cistercians and regular canons, the expansion of the *vita apostolica* toward preaching — with religious communities as the embodiment, not the negation, of medieval society. Robinson (Ch. 11, institutions of the church 1073–1216) on the resulting papal monarchy: the pope "like a king in a kingdom," the cardinals, legates, and Lateran councils, and the crystallisation of *vicarius Christi* and *plenitudo potestatis*. Per the editors, this whole transformation should not be reduced to the papal–imperial duel; the blow-by-blow Gregory VII–Henry IV narrative is reserved for Part 2 (Cycles 5/8).

Created: `processes/gregorian-reform.md`, `events/investiture-contest.md`, `actors/gregory-vii.md`, `concepts/dictatus-papae.md`, `concepts/papal-monarchy.md`, `processes/medieval-monastic-revival.md`, `actors/cistercian-order.md`. Updated: `actors/cluny.md` (eleventh-century apogee; sources 1→2), `periods/high-middle-ages.md` (church reform in Major Developments), and the source page's pages list. Forward links left for the Part 2 narrative: [[papacy]], [[henry-iv-holy-roman-emperor]], [[salian-dynasty]], [[urban-ii]].

## 2026-06-12 section | NCMH Vol. 4 — c. 1024–c. 1198 (Luscombe & Riley-Smith eds., 2004) | Section: Cycle 1 — Society, economy, law, knighthood (Part 1, Chs 1–7) | Pages created: 7 | Pages updated: 3

Began the Large-Volume ingest of NCMH IV. Cycle 1 covers the editors' Introduction and Part 1's secular-thematic chapters: Fossier on the rural economy and demographic growth (the European "take-off," population more than doubling 1000–1300, Domesday Book, assarting, the agricultural revolution, improving diet/longevity); Keene on towns and trade (the commercial/urban revival across the 4,000-km European span, set against a still-superior Islamic Mediterranean — the relationship not reversed for half a millennium); Reynolds on government and community (her landmark critique of the "feudal society" stereotype — government as public authority not mere personal lordship, community as pervasive *gentes/nationes/populi* rather than the achievement of a twelfth-century "communal movement"); Landau on the development of law (the revival of Roman law at Bologna under Irnerius, the *Authentica Habita* of 1155, Gratian's *Decretum* and canon law, the decline of the ordeal, the divergence of English common law under Henry II); Flori on knightly society (castle and knight as the twin pillars, the *milites*, dubbing, the conversion of a military function into a chivalric order, *milites sancti Petri*); and Hehl on war, peace and the Christian order (the dual evaluation of war, and the eleventh-century transformation by which the Church took up the direction of violence and war became a path to salvation — the conceptual road to the Crusade). The editors' organising thesis — the eleventh–twelfth centuries as "a unique fulcrum" of expansion and consolidation — is captured across the period and process pages.

Created: `processes/medieval-agricultural-revolution.md`, `concepts/feudalism.md`, `controversies/feudalism-construct-debate.md`, `concepts/chivalry.md`, `concepts/three-orders.md`, `concepts/just-war.md`, `concepts/ius-commune.md`. Updated: `processes/commercial-revolution.md` (sources 1→2), `concepts/peace-of-god.md` (sources 1→2), `periods/high-middle-ages.md` (the European socioeconomic/legal "take-off"; sources 2→3).
