## 2026-06-12 ingest-complete | NCMH Vol. 4 — c. 1024–c. 1198 (Luscombe & Riley-Smith eds., 2004) | Total pages created: 53 | Total pages updated: 11 | Sections processed: 9

All nine section cycles processed across both physical parts (Part 1's 19 thematic chapters in Cycles 1–4; Part 2's 23 regional chapters in Cycles 5–9), plus the source page. The Step 3 cross-section synthesis pass is complete and the **Volume Synthesis Note** is written in `sources/luscombe-ncmh-v4-2004.md`. This volume is the reference backbone for the **central Middle Ages** (c. 1024–c. 1198) and **fills the wiki's one remaining NCMH gap**, between Reuter's NCMH III (ends c. 1024) and Abulafia's NCMH V (begins c. 1198); it was supplied by the user as EPUBs after being flagged absent, converted to Markdown, and ingested.

Whole-volume connections drawn in the synthesis: (1) the **complementary Part 1 (themes) / Part 2 (narrative) architecture** — reconnecting the Gregorian Reform (thematic) with the Investiture-Contest/Salian narrative, the crusade-as-idea with the crusader states and Hattin, the law/schools revival with the universities and Angevin/Capetian government, and the communes with the Lombard League and Legnano; (2) a **divergence of monarchies** — the reform crisis weakening German kingship and the failure of Barbarossa in Italy, against the institutional strengthening of Angevin England and Capetian France, crystallised in the twin 1204 catastrophes (the Angevin empire dismembered by Philip Augustus; Byzantium by the Fourth Crusade); (3) the editorial insistence that **Christendom ≠ Europe** (the eastern churches, al-Andalus, and Ashkenazi/Sephardic Jewry as integral), shadowed by the hardening "persecuting society" (the 1096 massacres, anti-heresy, 1204). Cross-volume: NCMH IV extends NCMH III's anti-*mutation* scepticism into Reynolds's dismantling of "feudalism" itself, decentres the Investiture Contest (paralleling the wiki's transformation-not-catastrophe framing of Rome's fall), and hands its terminal events (1204; the Angevin collapse) forward to NCMH V. Step 4: both EPUB source files deleted after the converted `.md` (Part 1 and Part 2 in `raw/Markdown/`) were confirmed on disk. index.md catalog entry and statistics refreshed (total → 1110; High-Middle-Ages period upgraded to 3 ingested sources and its dangling [[crusades]], [[medieval-university]], [[reconquista]] links resolved). Git commit and push follow.

## 2026-06-12 section | History of West Africa Vol. I (Ajayi & Crowder, eds., 1971) | Section: Cycle 1 — Land, prehistory, stateless societies (Chs 1–3) | Pages created: 3 | Pages updated: 4

Began the Large-Volume ingest of *History of West Africa* Vol. I, the standard nationalist-era (Ibadan-school) reference, completing the wiki's Gap-2 (sub-Saharan Africa) West-Africa block after the GHA series. Cycle 1 covers the three thematic introductory chapters. Mabogunje (Ch. 1, land & peoples) supplies the regional framework — the east–west succession of ecological zones (Sahara/Sahel/savannah/forest/coast), the ethnographic survey of peoples (Mande, Senegambian, Songhay, Mossi, Hausa, Kanuri, the forest peoples, Fulani), and the "movement south" thesis (geography permissive, not deterministic). Shaw (Ch. 2, prehistory) on West Africa's independent tropical domesticates (yams, African rice, fonio, sorghum/millet), the Saharan-desiccation barrier that meant sub-Saharan Africa skipped the Bronze Age and re-entered wider contact through iron, the Nok culture as the earliest sub-Saharan ironworking (with the Meroe-vs-Carthage diffusion debate), and the Igbo-Ukwu/cire-perdue bronze tradition. Horton (Ch. 3, stateless societies) — the conceptually pivotal chapter — defines statelessness (four-point definition; ~35 million West Africans), the segmentary-lineage typology (Type 1 Tiv/Ibo vs territorial Types 2/3), and the indigenous-development model of state formation (continuity not rupture; trade as engine; rejection of the Hamitic Hypothesis).

Created: `concepts/stateless-societies.md`, `controversies/hamitic-hypothesis-african-states.md`, `places/west-africa.md` (the regional anchor for the whole volume). Updated: `actors/nok-culture.md` (sources 2→3; added the Saharan-barrier/no-Bronze-Age framing and the diffusion-vs-independence revisionism, Shaw favouring diffusion in 1966 against the later CWH IV/GHA II independence consensus), `processes/state-formation.md` (sources 4→5; Horton's bottom-up West African model), `processes/early-african-food-production.md` (sources 2→3; Shaw's West African indigenous domesticates), `concepts/griot-oral-tradition.md` (sources 1→2; the states-vs-stateless oral-tradition asymmetry as a source bias). Forward links left for later cycles: [[niger-delta-city-states]], [[mossi-states]], igbo-people, ife, fulani.

## 2026-06-12 section | History of West Africa Vol. I (Ajayi & Crowder, eds., 1971) | Section: Cycle 2 — The Sudanic states to the 16th c. (Chs 4–6) | Pages created: 2 | Pages updated: 10

Cycle 2 covers the great Sahelian/Sudanic states. Levtzion (Ch. 4, early Western Sudan to 1500) on his triad of trade–states–Islam: Ghana/Wagadu (the snake legend; al-Bakri's 1067 portrait; matrilineal succession; gold-nugget monopoly), the Almoravid movement (Abdallah ibn Yasin; conquest of Ghana 1076–7) and its aftermath, Sosso/Sumanguru, Mali (the Ibn Khaldun dynastic chronicle; Sundiata=Mari-Djata; Sakura; Mansa Musa/Sulayman; the able-then-weak dynastic cycle), Takrur (earliest Islamised Sudanese state) and Dyara, the Timbuktu/Jenne/Walata entrepôt shifts, and the beginnings of Songhay (Sorko fishermen; Gao/Kukiya; Dya & Sonni dynasties; al-Mahallabi's Muslim king of Gao 985). His sharp comparative thesis: Mali endured longest because its capital lay in the savannah, not the contested Sahil — Ghana and Songhay were each destroyed suddenly by northern powers, Mali only gradually by a local one (Bambara). H.F.C. Smith (Ch. 5, Central Sudan) on Kanem: the forging of the Kanuri by Sefawa intermarriage, divine kingship (the mune), the first empire under Dunama Dibbalemi (control of Fezzan), the Bulala wars and flight to Bornu, 'Ali Gaji's restoration; and on the Hausa, the birni (walled cosmopolitan city) as the engine of state formation from a stateless society (sarki/sarkin kasa; feudal masu sarauta). Smith and Levtzion both reject the Hamitic Hypothesis. Hunwick (Ch. 6, 16th c.) on the Songhay apogee under Askia Muhammad (admin; caliph of Takrur; al-Maghili) and Dawud, and the Moroccan invasion of 1591 (Taghaza salt-tax casus belli; Judar Pasha; Tondibi; firearms decisive).

Created: `actors/takrur.md`, `events/moroccan-invasion-of-songhay-1591.md` (Tondibi, with the pashalik aftermath reserved for Cycle 5/Ch 12). Updated: `actors/ghana-empire.md` (3→4; Wagadu legend & al-Bakri; flagged a [CONTRADICTION] — Levtzion affirms the 1076 Almoravid conquest, GHA III on the same page revises it down), `actors/mali-empire.md` (3→4; Ibn Khaldun chronicle & the savannah-capital survival thesis), `actors/songhay-empire.md` (2→3; Sorko origins + 16th-c apogee), `actors/mansa-musa.md` (1→2; Gongo Musa, Dulcert 1339, griots belittle him), `actors/sundiata-keita.md` (1→2; Mari-Djata documentary cross-check), `actors/almoravids.md` (2→3; the Saharan birth & southern front), `actors/kanem-bornu.md` (2→3; forging the Kanuri, first empire, flight to Bornu), `actors/hausa-city-states.md` (3→4; the birni & state formation), `processes/trans-saharan-trade.md` (2→3; routes, silent trade, entrepôt shifts), `processes/islamization-of-africa.md` (1→2; the Sudanic social-gradient pattern). Forward links left: [[western-sudan-arma-pashalik]], [[ahmad-baba]], [[niger-delta-city-states]] (Ch 12/Cycle 5; Ch 8/Cycle 3).

## 2026-06-12 section | History of West Africa Vol. I (Ajayi & Crowder, eds., 1971) | Section: Cycle 3 — The Atlantic era: trade, delta, forest (Chs 7–9) | Pages created: 1 | Pages updated: 3

Cycle 3 covers the Atlantic-facing chapters. Curtin (Ch. 7, the Atlantic slave trade 1600–1800 — author of the 1969 *Census*): the trade as the largest pre-19th-c. intercontinental migration and the labour engine of the South Atlantic System; the Mediterranean genealogy of the slave plantation (Cyprus/Crete → Sicily/Iberia → Madeira/São Tomé → Brazil/Caribbean); the ~10 million landed (a scaling-down of older 20-million guesses); and the categorical difference between West African/ancient *social* slavery and American *plantation* (dehumanised, racially-badged) slavery. Alagoa (Ch. 8, the Niger Delta states): the Ijo city-states (Bonny, Kalabari, Okrika, Nembe), ancient monarchy transformed by the Atlantic trade — Dike's "city-states"/Jones's "trading states" — the amanyanabo boosted by comey, and above all the War Canoe House (wari), an "open" corporation in which a bought slave could rise to House head, with ekine/sekiapu societies enforcing acculturation. Akinjogbin (Ch. 9, Oyo & Dahomey): Agaja's conquest of Whydah and the slave-trade royal monopoly, the 1730 treaty making Dahomey tributary to Oyo, Tegbesu's absolutism (Ilari, Yovogan, succession law), the "Amazons," and his thesis that the slave trade was economically self-destructive; for Oyo, the Gaha–Alafin struggle reframed as an expansion-vs-exploitation policy dispute, won by the trader-Alafin Abiodun (1774).

Created: `actors/niger-delta-city-states.md` (resolving the forward links from stateless-societies and state-formation). Updated: `processes/atlantic-slave-trade.md` (4→5; the South Atlantic System & Mediterranean roots, comparative slavery), `actors/oyo-empire.md` (2→3; Gaha–Alafin & Abiodun), `actors/dahomey.md` (1→2; Agaja, the 1730 treaty, Tegbesu, the slave-trade-as-trap thesis).

## 2026-06-12 section | History of West Africa Vol. I (Ajayi & Crowder, eds., 1971) | Section: Cycle 4 — Mossi/Akan & the Western Atlantic coast (Chs 10–11) | Pages created: 2 | Pages updated: 2

Cycle 4 covers the Voltaic and forest states and the Senegambian coast. Wilks (Ch. 10, Mossi & Akan 1500–1800): the Mossi cavalry complex (southern Mamprussi/Dagomba/Nanumba, northern Tenkodogo/Wagadugu/Yatenga/Fada N'Gurma; descent from Na Gbewa; Mande-mercenary origins; the 15th–16th-c. wars with Songhay; the nam/tengdana polarity and integration of autochthones), and the Akan world — matriclans as integration device, the gold-then-slave trade as engine, Gonja (Mande-founded, three estates, the Salaga kola entrepôt), Akwamu (Abrade traders → dual trade-/tribute-state → 1730 collapse), Denkyira (gold; defeated by Asante 1699–1701), Fante (coastal "republics," English middlemen), and the rise of Asante out of this matrix. Suret-Canale (Ch. 11, Western Atlantic coast): the Sape kingdom and Mane/Sumba invasion; the Baga-Temne-Susu peoples; and the 18th-c. Fula-Islamic theocracies of Futa Jallon, Futa Toro and Bundu (the Umma replacing the tribal monarchy) — paired with the Abdoulaye Ly "contradiction" thesis that the Atlantic trade built states inland while causing political regression and re-segmentation on the coast.

Created: `actors/mossi-states.md`, `actors/akan-states.md` (Bono, Gonja, Akwamu, Denkyira, Fante). Updated: `actors/asante-empire.md` (1→2; the rise out of the Akan world / defeat of Denkyira), `processes/west-african-jihads.md` (1→2; the 18th-c. Futa Jallon/Toro/Bundu precursors and the slave-trade "contradiction" thesis). Resolved the forward link [[mossi-states]] left from Cycle 1.

## 2026-06-12 section | History of West Africa Vol. I (Ajayi & Crowder, eds., 1971) | Section: Cycle 5 — The Sudan in the Atlantic era (Chs 12–13) + synthesis | Pages created: 2 | Pages updated: 2

Final cycle. Willis (Ch. 12, the Western Sudan from the Moroccan invasion 1591 to al-Mukhtar al-Kunti 1811): the Arma/Ruma soldier-caste and the Makhzan administration of Timbuktu; the slide into functional independence from Morocco (independent basha 1612; own name in the khutba 1660); the structural weakness of the rotating basha office and the fatal mercenary dependence; Willis's revision of the "time of troubles"/decline-of-learning thesis (administrative-military decline, not civilisational collapse; Ahmad Baba's capture as political expediency, not religious persecution); and the closing Kunta clerical realignment under Sidi al-Mukhtar al-Kunti (a zawaya Qadiri brotherhood becoming the supreme spiritual power of the Middle Niger after c. 1770 — prefiguring the jihads). Adeleye (Ch. 13, Hausaland & Bornu 1600–1800): the movement toward larger polities, Kano's military development (the madawaki), Kano–Katsina rivalry, and Bornu's might — the Hausa on the eve of the Sokoto jihad.

Created: `actors/western-sudan-arma-pashalik.md`, `actors/ahmad-baba.md` (resolving the forward links from the Moroccan-invasion event and songhay-empire). Updated: `events/moroccan-invasion-of-songhay-1591.md` (the Arma aftermath & Kunta realignment, replacing the reserved note), `actors/hausa-city-states.md` (4→5; Adeleye on 1600–1800). Step 3 synthesis pass complete; the Volume Synthesis Note is written in the source page.

## 2026-06-12 ingest | Ibn Battuta in Black Africa (Hamdun & King, trans., 1975/1994) | Late Middle Ages | east-africa, swahili-coast, west-africa, sahel, sahara | Pages created: 2 | Pages updated: 6

Standard single-pass ingest of the primary source — translated selections from Ibn Battuta's *Rihla* covering his two sub-Saharan journeys, with a Ross Dunn foreword. The **only 14th-c. eyewitness account of both the East African city-states and the Mali empire**. East Africa (c. 1329/1331): Zaila, Mogadishu (the "shaikh" Abu Bakr; the sunbuq host-broker custom; the council where the qadi judges sharia and the ministers the rest), Mombasa, Kilwa (Sultan al-Hasan Abu al-Mawahib, "father of gifts," and his razzias on the Zunuj). West Africa (1352–54): the Sahara crossing (Sijilmasa, the Taghaza salt mine, the takshif guide, salt-as-currency), Walata and the matrilineal/un-secluded Massufa, the Mali capital under Mansa Sulaiman (the cupola, the banbi dais, gold regalia, the interpreter-bard Dugha, the bird-costumed pre-Islamic jali poets, the dusting/self-abasement, the co-queen Qasa), Timbuktu (al-Sahili's grave), Gao (cowrie currency), and Takadda (copper-rod currency traded to Gobir and Bornu under its veiled king Idris). Ibn Battuta's famous double-edged verdict — praising Mali's justice, security, and Qur'anic devotion while deploring women's freedom, nudity, and the dusting rituals — read critically as the view of a Maliki qadi judging the Sudan by a Middle-Eastern patriarchal yardstick.

Created: `actors/ibn-battuta.md`, `sources/ibn-battuta-black-africa.md` (resolving the dangling [[ibn-battuta-black-africa]] links from mali-empire, sub-saharan-africa, griot-oral-tradition, niane-sundiata). Updated: `actors/mali-empire.md` (4→5; the eyewitness court of Mansa Sulaiman), `concepts/griot-oral-tradition.md` (2→3; the earliest external notice of the jali, attested "from before Islam"), `places/swahili-coast.md` (7→8; the 14th-c. coastal cities), `actors/kanem-bornu.md` (3→4; Bornu's veiled king Idris & the Takadda copper trade), `processes/trans-saharan-trade.md` (3→4; the desert crossing eyewitness), `actors/mansa-musa.md` (2→3; remembered for generosity). Source `.md` filed to `raw/3. Middle Ages/`. index.md banner + statistics refreshed (total → 1123). **Completes the Outstanding-Sources work for Critical Gap 2 (Sub-Saharan Africa).** Git commit and push follow.

## 2026-06-12 section | A History of India Vol. I (Romila Thapar, 1966) | Section: Cycle 1 — Foundations & the Vedic age (Chs 1–2) | Pages created: 2 | Pages updated: 3

Began the Large-Volume ingest of Thapar Vol. I, the standard academic narrative of India to 1526 and the structural backbone for Gap 3 (South Asia). Cycle 1 covers the two foundational chapters. Ch. 1 (The Antecedents): Thapar's historiographical framing — against the colonial "stagnant/spiritual East" myth (Macaulay vs. German romanticism), against the nationalist "Golden Age," against the misleading Hindu/Muslim periodization — in favour of a social-economic reading where caste, guilds and village councils (not "Oriental despotism") localise power; and the archaeological background, above all the Indus/Harappan civilization (Mohenjo-daro, Harappa, the planned citadel-cities, granaries, the undeciphered seals, Mesopotamian trade, decline c. 1500 BCE). Ch. 2 (The Impact of Aryan Culture): the Indo-Aryan migration/acculturation (Aryan = linguistic not racial), the Vedic corpus and epics, the pastoral→agrarian shift with iron (c. 800 BCE), tribal kingship checked by the sabha/samiti and the rise of divine kingship, the origins of caste (three classes → four varnas; varna = "colour"; the Dasas as shudras; varna-as-theory vs. jati-as-practice), and Vedic religion (Indra/Agni/Varuna, sacrifice, the Creation Hymn, asceticism and the Upanishads, Karma/Dharma) fusing with surviving Harappan fertility cults toward Hinduism.

Created: `actors/indus-valley-civilization.md`, `periods/vedic-period.md` (regional sub-period anchor). Updated: `concepts/caste-varna.md` (1→2; Thapar's colour-origin and varna-theory/jati-practice argument; fixed the [[vedic-age]]→[[vedic-period]] link), `controversies/indo-european-origins.md` (added the South Asian "Aryan question" — migration not invasion, Painted-Grey Ware, the anti-"Out-of-India" secular framing), `concepts/hinduism.md` (1→2; the Vedic + pre-Aryan double roots; fixed [[vedic-age]] link). Forward links left: [[south-asia]], [[magadha]] (Cycle 2). Note: a few legacy pages still link [[vedic-age]] — to reconcile with the new [[vedic-period]] in a later lint.

## 2026-06-12 section | A History of India Vol. I (Romila Thapar, 1966) | Section: Cycle 2 — Magadha to the Mauryas (Chs 3–4) | Pages created: 1 | Pages updated: 4

Cycle 2 covers the rise of states and the first empire. Ch. 3 (Republics and Kingdoms, c. 600–321 BCE): the gana-sangha republics vs. the Gangetic monarchies; the heterodox sects (Buddha/Shakya, Mahavira/Jnatrika) and the Buddhist social-contract (Mahasammata) theory of the state; the rise of Magadha (Bimbisara, Ajatashatru), the Shishunagas and the low-born Nandas (first empire-builders); urbanisation and the merchant/guild class; the emergence of untouchables; Achaemenid contact (Gandhara, Taxila, Kharoshthi, the inspiration for rock inscriptions) and Alexander's historically-negligible campaign. Ch. 4 (The Emergence of Empire, 321–185 BCE): the Mauryan centralized bureaucracy (Arthashastra superintendents, espionage, provincial structure, Megasthenes' Pataliputra) and Thapar's reinterpretation of Ashoka's Dhamma as a secular, non-sectarian "attitude of social responsibility" — NOT a synonym for Buddhism — plus her economic-pressure (coin-debasement) account of Mauryan decline.

Created: `concepts/gana-sangha.md` (the early Indian republics + the Buddhist social-contract theory). Updated: `actors/ashoka.md` (1→2; Dhamma ≠ Buddhism), `actors/mauryan-empire.md` (1→2; Thapar's centralized-bureaucracy reading flagged against CWH IV's "not a monolith"; the decline thesis), `actors/magadha.md` (1→2; why Magadha won + the Nandas), `concepts/buddhism.md` (4→5; the sects' social base in the republics and merchant class). Forward links left: [[south-asia]] (place anchor), the southern dynasties and Guptas (Cycle 4).

## 2026-06-12 section | A History of India Vol. I (Romila Thapar, 1966) | Section: Cycle 3 — Between the empires (Chs 5–6) | Pages created: 3 | Pages updated: 3

Cycle 3 covers the post-Mauryan centuries (c. 200 BCE–300 CE). Ch. 5 (Disintegration of Empire): the succession of north-western intrusive powers — Bactrian/Indo-Greeks, Shakas (Scythians displaced by the Yueh-chi; the satrap Rudradaman's Junagarh inscription), and Kushanas (Kujula, Vima, Kanishka, the Shaka Era of 78 CE, the Fourth Buddhist Council, missions to China) — the Satavahanas of the Deccan (the north–south bridge; Gautamiputra's defeat of the Shakas), and the Sangam-age Tamil south (Cheras/Cholas/Pandyas, Sangam literature, Murugan, Roman trade); plus the trade routes (the Royal Highway/Grand Trunk Road, the monsoon, the Old Silk Road). Ch. 6 (Rise of the Mercantile Community): guilds (shreni, the Nasik inscription, guild-as-banker), the money economy, the Roman trade with South India (the Periplus, Arikamedu, the bullion drain Pliny decried), the Indianization of Southeast Asia (merchants and brahmans, Kaundinya in Cambodia), Sino-Indian Buddhist contact (White Horse Monastery, the oasis monasteries), Indo-Hellenic exchange (Gandhara art, Buddha–Christ parallels), and the social strain of assimilating foreigners ("fallen kshatriyas," Manu's law code).

Created: `actors/satavahana-empire.md`, `actors/sangam-age-south-india.md`, `processes/indianization-of-southeast-asia.md` (a foundational Gap-3/Southeast Asia page). Updated: `actors/kushan-empire.md` (1→2; the Greek→Shaka→Kushana succession & the "fallen kshatriya" device), `processes/indian-ocean-trade.md` (2→3; the classical Roman trade), `concepts/mahayana-buddhism.md` (1→2; the Kushan/Gandhara context). Forward links left: [[chola-empire]] (Cycle 4), angkor-khmer, periplus-maris-erythraei, [[south-asia]].

## 2026-06-12 section | A History of India Vol. I (Romila Thapar, 1966) | Section: Cycle 4 — The Classical pattern & the South (Chs 7–9) | Pages created: 3 | Pages updated: 2

Cycle 4 covers the Gupta "classical" age and the rise of the peninsular south. Ch. 7 (Evolution of the 'Classical' Pattern, 300–700): the Guptas (Chandra Gupta I; Samudra Gupta's Allahabad eulogy and the breaking of the western tribal republics — "caste's victory over tribe"; Chandra Gupta II/Vikramaditya, Kalidasa, Fa Hsien, the Vakataka marriage alliance; the Huna threat) — with Thapar's double qualification of the "Golden Age" label (elite, northern, less centralized than the Mauryas); and Harsha of Kanauj (606–647, Bana, Hsuan Tsang). Plus the religious developments from Ch. 6: the Hinayana/Mahayana Buddhist schism (Nagarjuna) and the making of classical Hinduism (the Brahma–Vishnu–Shiva trinity, Vishnu's avatars, Shiva from Rudra/Murugan, the Epics/Puranas/Gita as scripture, bhakti). Chs. 8–9 (the South, 500–1300): the geographically-driven Chalukya–Pallava–Pandya conflict, the Pallava "Tamil personality" (Mahabalipuram), the Rashtrakutas (Amoghavarsha), Shankara's Advaita and Ramanuja's Vaishnavism, the Tamil village-assembly (sabha) self-government, and the Chola maritime empire (Rajaraja, Rajendra's Srivijaya expedition, centralized temple-state).

Created: `actors/chola-empire.md`, `actors/pallava-chalukya-rashtrakuta.md`, `actors/harsha.md`. Updated: `actors/gupta-empire.md` (1→2; the "classical age for whom?" critique, the land-grant germ of feudalism), `concepts/hinduism.md` (re-touch; the trinity/avatar/Gita synthesis and the southern Shankara/Ramanuja/Tamil-saint developments). Forward links left: [[delhi-sultanate]], [[rajputs]], [[indian-feudalism]], [[bhakti-movement]], [[srivijaya]] (Cycles 5–6).

## 2026-06-12 section | A History of India Vol. I (Romila Thapar, 1966) | Section: Cycle 5 — Regional states & the coming of Islam (Chs 10–11) | Pages created: 3 | Pages updated: 1

Cycle 5 covers the fragmented post-Gupta north and the first Muslim impact. Ch. 10 (Beginnings of Regional States, 700–1200): the tripartite struggle over Kanauj (Pratiharas, Palas, Rashtrakutas); the peripheral states (Kashmir — Kalhana's Rajatarangini; Nepal; Assam); the Rajputs (uncertain/foreign origin, the Agnikula fire-myth, the manufactured kshatriya status, probable Huna descent, the four clans); Mahmud of Ghazni's plunder-raids culminating in Somnath (1026); and Muhammad Ghuri's conquest (the battles of Tarain 1191/92, Prithviraja, the reasons for Turkish success — cavalry, tactics, plunder-motive vs. Indian exhaustion). Ch. 11 (Feudalism in the Regional States): Thapar's "Indian feudalism" — the samanta hierarchy built on hereditary land/revenue grants, sub-infeudation diffusing income from king and cultivator, a closed low-trade subsistence-village economy, and the depressed peasantry — the structural disunity behind the failure to resist the Turks.

Created: `actors/rajputs.md`, `processes/indian-feudalism.md` (resolving the forward link from gupta-empire), `actors/mahmud-of-ghazni.md`. Updated: `concepts/buddhism.md` (re-touch; Thapar's account of Buddhism's Indian decline — monastic affluence/isolation, lost merchant patronage, Shankara's competition, absorption of the Buddha as Vishnu's avatar). Forward link left: [[delhi-sultanate]] (Cycle 6).

## 2026-06-12 section | A History of India Vol. I (Romila Thapar, 1966) | Section: Cycle 6 — The Delhi Sultanate & assimilation (Chs 12–14) | Pages created: 4 | Pages updated: 0

Final cycle. Ch. 12 (Re-alignment of Regional Kingdoms, 1200–1526): the Delhi Sultanate's five dynasties (Slave/Mamluk — Iltutmish, Balban; Khalji — Ala-ud-din at the apogee, with grant-revocation, the half-share land tax, market/price controls, espionage; Tughluq — Muhammad bin Tughluq, Firuz Shah; Sayyid; Lodi), the iqta/muqti administration and Sharia/jaziyah taxation (Thapar noting conversions *reduced* the jaziyah, so Sultans had little fiscal motive to force them), and Timur's 1398 sack. Ch. 13 (Assimilation on Trial): the Sufis (the Chishti order, the pir, contact with peasants/artisans, friction with the ulema) and the Bhakti movement — its Tamil devotional roots, its Sufi coalescence, its lower-caste/artisan/vernacular character, and its great figures Kabir (the weaver who equated Hindu and Muslim God) and Nanak (the Adi Granth; the seed of Sikhism). Ch. 14 (The South Conforms): Vijayanagara and the Bahmani kingdom, both born of the Sultanate's Deccan retreat (Harihara and Bukka, the convert-princes who reverted to Hinduism), their Krishna-frontier rivalry, and Thapar's scepticism toward the "Hindu revival" reading of Vijayanagara.

Created: `actors/delhi-sultanate.md`, `processes/bhakti-movement.md`, `actors/vijayanagara-bahmani.md`, plus the `places/south-asia.md` regional anchor (resolving the many forward [[south-asia]] links). Step 3 synthesis pass complete; the Volume Synthesis Note is written in the source page.

## 2026-06-12 section | A History of India Vol. II (Percival Spear, 1965) | Section: Cycle 1 — The Mughal Empire (Chs 1–5) | Pages created: 3 | Pages updated: 2

Began the Large-Volume ingest of Spear Vol. II, the continuation of Thapar Vol. I from 1526 — the Gap-3 backbone past the Mughal threshold (and a slice of Gap 1, modern India). Cycle 1 covers the Mughal empire. Ch. 1 (Coming of the Mughals): fragmented India c. 1520 (Lodi Afghans, Rajputs/Rana Sanga, the post-Bahmani Deccan sultanates, Vijayanagar, the arriving Portuguese); Babur (Timurid, Panipat 1526, Khanua 1527, the memoirs). Chs. 2–4 (Akbar; the Mughal Empire; the Great Mughals): Humayun and Sher Shah Sur's administrative blueprint; Akbar's four works (conquest; the Rajput/Hindu partnership and jizya abolition; the mansabdar service; imperial sanctity / the Din-i-Ilahi read as a cult of monarch) — Spear ranking Akbar with Ashoka; Jahangir, Shah Jahan (the Taj), and Spear's revisionist Aurangzeb (efficient not merely bigoted; the two-half reign; the draining Deccan/Maratha war); and the 17th-c. seeds of the future — Sirhindi's Muslim revivalism ("the germ of Pakistan"), the militarisation of the Sikhs, and the rise of the Marathas. Spear's organising thesis: the Mughal–British continuity.

Created: `actors/mughal-empire.md`, `actors/akbar.md`, `actors/aurangzeb.md`. Updated: `actors/delhi-sultanate.md` (1→2; the Mughal sequel & the Sultanate→Mughal continuity), `processes/bhakti-movement.md` (1→2; the Mughal-era vernacular flowering — Tulsidas/Chaitanya — and the Sikh militarisation). Forward links left: [[maratha-empire]], [[english-east-india-company]] enrich, [[indian-rebellion-1857]] (Cycles 2–3).

## 2026-06-12 section | A History of India Vol. II (Percival Spear, 1965) | Section: Cycle 2 — Decline & the rise of British power (Chs 6–9) | Pages created: 2 | Pages updated: 2

Cycle 2 covers the Mughal collapse and the Company's rise. Ch. 6 (Decline, Collapse, Confusion): the post-1707 disintegration — weak emperors, Nadir Shah's sack of Delhi (1739), the successor states (Hyderabad, Awadh, Bengal), the Marathas and Sikhs, and the Third Battle of Panipat (1761). The Marathas (from Ch. 4): Sivaji's rise (guerrilla war, Afzal Khan, the sack of Surat 1664), Maratha nationalism welding Brahmin and Sudra, and its souring into a hated "Maratha imperialism" defeated before it began. Ch. 5 (Europeans and Mughal India): the Portuguese strong-point empire (Albuquerque, Goa) and its sterile cultural legacy; the Dutch (Amboyna 1623); the English East India Company (1600), driven to India as a "second best," schooled by enforced competition, the Mughals' maritime auxiliary; the French (Dupleix). Chs. 7–9 (the English in Bengal): Plassey (1757), Clive and the puppet Mir Jafar, the "financial bleeding of Bengal," Buxar (1764), the diwani grant (1765) making the Company the Mughal revenue agent, and Warren Hastings giving the Company-state administrative shape.

Created: `actors/maratha-empire.md` (resolving the [[maratha-empire]] forward links), `events/battle-of-plassey-1757.md`. Updated: `actors/english-east-india-company.md` (1→2; Spear's "how the Company won" — the competition-as-schooling thesis, Plassey→diwani→Hastings), `actors/mughal-empire.md` (re-touch; the decline section). Forward links left: [[british-raj]], [[indian-rebellion-1857]], bengal (Cycle 3).

## 2026-06-12 section | A History of India Vol. II (Percival Spear, 1965) | Section: Cycle 3 — Conquest & consolidation of the Raj (Chs 10–12) | Pages created: 2 | Pages updated: 0

Cycle 3 covers the making of British India. Chs. 10–11 (The New Policy; Completion of Dominion): Cornwallis (the Europeanized covenanted service; the Permanent Settlement of Bengal 1793, which Spear judges harshly — the zamindar a rentier, the peasant a proletariat); Wellesley's subsidiary-alliance expansion; the anglicist turn (Bentinck's abolition of suttee 1829, Macaulay's 1835 Minute and English education, the orientalist–anglicist controversy); and Dalhousie's railways/universities plus the doctrine of lapse and annexations (Oudh 1856). Ch. 12 (The Imperial Heyday) opens with the 1857 Rebellion: the greased cartridges, Meerut, Delhi and Bahadur Shah, the Kanpur/Lucknow sieges, Rani of Jhansi, Sikh loyalty, savage reprisals — and Spear's reading of it as a backward-looking "last convulsive protest against the coming of the west," not a nationalist war; then the Crown takeover (1858) and the post-Mutiny caution (respecting the established classes; the army recast; the silent shift of Indian hopes to the English-educated class).

Created: `events/indian-rebellion-1857.md` (resolving the many [[indian-rebellion-1857]] forward links), `actors/british-raj.md` (Cornwallis/Wellesley/Dalhousie, anglicism, the Crown Raj — with Spear's continuist/benign framing flagged against later drain/subaltern revisionism). Forward links left: [[indian-national-congress]], [[mahatma-gandhi]] (Cycle 4).

## 2026-06-12 section | A History of India Vol. II (Percival Spear, 1965) | Section: Cycle 4 — The national movement (Chs 13–16) | Pages created: 2 | Pages updated: 0

Cycle 4 covers the rise of Indian nationalism. Chs. 13–14 (cultural & political nationalism; Congress, Curzon, Reform): the 19th-c. religious-cultural renaissance (Ram Mohan Roy & the Brahmo Samaj, the Arya Samaj, Vivekananda) as the seedbed; the founding of the Indian National Congress (1885) as a moderate petitioning body; Curzon's Partition of Bengal (1905) and the Swadeshi agitation; and the moderate–extremist split (Gokhale the Gladstonian liberal vs. Tilak the Hindu-revivalist advocate of direct action). Chs. 15–16 (WWI & the Great Leap; Mahatma): the post-WWI ferment (the Rowlatt bills, the Amritsar/Jallianwala Bagh massacre 1919, the Khilafat alliance), and Gandhi — his ideas (satya, ahimsa, satyagraha, the village-republic ideal, the Harijan cause) and his campaigns (Non-cooperation 1920–22, called off after Chauri Chaura; the 1930 Salt March), and his genius for dramatising issues to a mass/peasant audience.

Created: `actors/indian-national-congress.md`, `actors/mahatma-gandhi.md` (resolving their forward links). Forward links left: [[indian-independence-movement]], [[partition-of-india-1947]], [[jawaharlal-nehru]] (Cycle 5).

## 2026-06-12 section | A History of India Vol. II (Percival Spear, 1965) | Section: Cycle 5 — Independence & after (Chs 17–21) | Pages created: 4 | Pages updated: 1

Final cycle. Chs. 17–18 (the 1935 Act & WWII; the Pakistan Movement): Jinnah's turn from "ambassador of unity" to the two-nation theory, the revival of the Muslim League and the 1940 Pakistan demand, Quit India (1942), the Cripps failure. Chs. 19–21 (Independence & Consolidation; Nehru's India; epilogue): the five figures (Gandhi, Nehru, Jinnah, Wavell, Mountbatten), the Cabinet Mission's failure as "the point of no return," Direct Action Day and the slide to communal civil war, the Mountbatten plan and the Radcliffe boundary, independence and Partition (15 Aug 1947, Nehru's "tryst with destiny"); then Patel & Menon's integration of the 362 princely states (Junagadh, Hyderabad 1948, the disputed Kashmir), the secular Republican Constitution (1950), the Planning Commission and Five-Year Plans, and non-alignment.

Created: `events/partition-of-india-1947.md`, `actors/muhammad-ali-jinnah.md`, `actors/jawaharlal-nehru.md`, `processes/indian-independence-movement.md` (resolving the remaining forward links). Updated: `places/south-asia.md` (1→2; extended the anchor into the Mughal/British/independence era). Step 3 synthesis pass complete; the Volume Synthesis Note is written in the source page.

## 2026-06-12 section | Angkor and the Khmer Civilization (Michael D. Coe, 2003) | Section: Cycle 1 — Land, people & the pre-Angkorian states (Chs 1–4) | Pages created: 2 | Pages updated: 1

Began the Khmer ingest — the wiki's first dedicated Southeast Asian source, opening the long-flagged Gap-3 SE Asia hole. Cycle 1 covers the setting and the pre-Angkorian states. Chs. 1–3: the French EFEO discovery/epigraphic tradition (Cœdès); the Lower Mekong environment and the monsoon-driven Tonle Sap (the Great Lake's annual flow-reversal — the basis of the later hydraulic civilization); wet rice; the Mon-Khmer/Austroasiatic Khmer people and language. Ch. 4: the Indianized delta states — "Funan" and its great maritime entrepôt Oc Eo (Roman/Indian/Chinese trade; the Antoninus Pius medallion; the earliest Sanskrit writing and Hindu cults in the Khmer area), and the inland shift to the stratified Zhenla kingdoms (Bhavavarman, Ishanavarman; Sambor Prei Kuk/Ishanapura; the worship of Shiva-linga, Vishnu, and Harihara) — the seedbed of the devaraja kingship.

Created: `actors/funan.md` (Funan/Oc Eo & Zhenla), `places/southeast-asia.md` (the regional anchor for the gap — monsoon, Indianization, mainland/island). Updated: `processes/indianization-of-southeast-asia.md` (1→2; the Khmer archaeological close-up — Oc Eo and the Zhenla temples). Forward links left: [[khmer-empire]], [[angkor]] (Cycle 2).

## 2026-06-12 section | Angkor and the Khmer Civilization (Michael D. Coe, 2003) | Section: Cycle 2 — The Khmer Empire of Angkor (Ch 5) | Pages created: 2 | Pages updated: 2

Cycle 2 covers the imperial heart of the book. The founding: Jayavarman II's consecration as chakravartin on Mahendraparvata (AD 802) and the institution of the devaraja ("god who is king") cult; Hariharalaya (Roluos) and the first barays/temple-mountains (Indravarman's Bakong); Yasovarman's move to Angkor. The hydraulic civilization (the Tonle Sap, the great barays, LiDAR-mapped Greater Angkor). The great kings: Suryavarman II (acc. 1113, Vishnu devotee, builder of Angkor Wat with its Churning-of-the-Sea-of-Milk reliefs; the Mannikka solar-cosmology thesis; Cœdès's funerary reading); the Cham sack of Angkor (1177); and the apogee under the Mahayana Buddhist Jayavarman VII (reconquest 1181, Angkor Thom and the Bayon's Avalokiteshvara face-towers, 102 hospitals, the suspension of the devaraja).

Created: `actors/khmer-empire.md` (resolving the [[khmer-empire]]/angkor-khmer forward-links), `actors/jayavarman-vii.md`. Updated: `places/angkor.md` (2→3; Coe's monumental/dynastic detail added to the existing CWH urbanism page), `concepts/mahayana-buddhism.md` (2→3; the Khmer royal Mahayana under Jayavarman VII). Reconciled the legacy angkor-khmer link → [[khmer-empire]] in indianization-of-southeast-asia.

## 2026-06-12 section | Angkor and the Khmer Civilization (Michael D. Coe, 2003) | Section: Cycle 3 — Khmer society, religion & decline (Chs 6–7) | Pages created: 1 | Pages updated: 2

Final cycle. Ch. 6 (Classic Khmer society): the rice-based, largely moneyless economy (barter in rice/cloth/Chinese goods; taxation in kind on everything), heavy reliance on slaves and corvée, the "hydraulic city" debate (Groslier's irrigation thesis vs. flood-retreat farming and the cosmological reading of the barays), the devaraja state religion, and the prominence of women in trade — much of it from Zhou Daguan's 1296–97 eyewitness account. Ch. 7 (post-Classic decline & transformation): the turn to (anti-monumental) Theravada Buddhism that stopped great-temple building, the rising Thai (Ayutthaya) sacking Angkor in the 15th c., hydraulic-system strain, and the shift of the Khmer centre to Phnom Penh.

Created: `concepts/devaraja.md` (the god-king cult). Updated: `actors/khmer-empire.md` (re-touch; society/economy/religion + a fuller Theravada/Thai decline) and `concepts/hinduism.md` (2→3; the Khmer devaraja as Hinduism's grandest SE Asian expression). Step 3 synthesis pass complete; the Volume Synthesis Note is written in the source page.

## 2026-06-12 section | Reid, Southeast Asia in the Age of Commerce, Vol. 1 | Section: Cycle 1 — The region & physical life (Chs I–II) | Pages created: 1 | Pages updated: 1

## 2026-06-12 section | Reid, Southeast Asia in the Age of Commerce, Vol. 1 | Section: Cycle 2 — Material & social life (Chs III–IV) | Pages created: 2 | Pages updated: 1

Created two concept pages from Reid's signature social analysis: `concepts/women-in-precolonial-southeast-asia.md` — the **autonomy-of-women** thesis (value of daughters; bride-wealth-to-the-bride not dowry; bilateral kinship, matrilocal residence, joint/equal-inheritance property defying Indian/Chinese/Islamic models; women dominating marketing & money-changing; female rulers & a female shahbandar; sexual autonomy incl. the male genital surgery read as a symptom of female power; the gender-dualism cosmology and the long erosion under the world religions) — and `concepts/debt-bondage-in-southeast-asia.md` — the **manpower-not-land** frame (power measured in followers; debt as the master-bond; the household/compound; warfare aimed at seizing people not territory, low battle casualties but devastating displacement; the stratified-but-fluid manpower state). Enriched `places/southeast-asia.md` with Ch. III material culture (the portable pile house vs. monumental temples; little dress differentiation; metalwork as "the key to power"; tin as the one metal export). Lieberman's mainland-administrative-integration critique flagged on both concept pages.

## 2026-06-12 section | Reid, Southeast Asia in the Age of Commerce, Vol. 2 | Section: Cycle 1 — The commercial boom (Ch I) | Pages created: 0 | Pages updated: 1

Enriched `processes/southeast-asian-age-of-commerce.md` with the economic dimension from Vol. 2 Ch. I — the "long sixteenth century" boom; pepper/cloves/nutmeg as Braudelian merchant-capital commodities originating in SE Asia (Maluku, Banda, Sumatra); spices as the measurable index of the trade cycle (leap c. 1400, crest c. 1640); the boom fed by New World & Japanese silver (the silk-for-silver entrepôt trade via Manila & Hoi An); "the sea trade created the age of commerce, the caravan trade followed"; and the turn as Asian entrepôts lost long-distance trade to the European beachheads (Melaka 1511, Manila 1571, Batavia 1619) and VOC price manipulation. ## 2026-06-12 section | Reid, Southeast Asia in the Age of Commerce, Vol. 2 | Section: Cycle 2 — The city and its commerce (Ch II) | Pages created: 2 | Pages updated: 1

Created `concepts/southeast-asian-port-polity.md` — the cosmopolitan entrepôt-city as the signature form of the age of commerce (the monsoon-entrepôt logic; the Funan/Srivijaya→Pasai/Melaka→Aceh-Banten→Batavia succession; Reid's striking thesis that SE Asian cities were BIGGER in 1600 than 1850 and that colonialism de-urbanised the region; and the urbanism-and-capitalism debate — the van Leur vs Meilink-Roelofsz argument, the absent impersonal capital institutions, the city=state coterminous, and security of property "against the whim of an arbitrary sovereign" as the decisive Europe/SE-Asia difference). Created `places/melaka.md` — the paradigmatic entrepôt (Pires's "city made for merchandise"; Parameswara c. 1400; the shahbandar system; the Islam springboard; Albuquerque's 1511 conquest that dispersed rather than captured the trade; Johor/Aceh successors; VOC 1641). Enriched `processes/indian-ocean-trade.md` (the SE Asian terminus & the age-of-commerce intensification; sources_ingested→4). Note: Vietnam flagged as the Confucian anti-commerce exception ("emphasize agriculture, commerce is peripheral").

## 2026-06-12 section | Reid, Southeast Asia in the Age of Commerce, Vol. 2 | Section: Cycle 3 — A religious revolution (Ch III) | Pages created: 1 | Pages updated: 2

Created `processes/islamization-of-southeast-asia.md` — the wiki's first dedicated treatment, framed within Reid's connected "religious revolution" thesis (Islam in the archipelago + Christianity in the Philippines/Vietnam + reformed Theravada on the mainland + Confucianism in Vietnam, all scriptural universalist orthodoxies displacing local animism, concentrated in the 1570–1630 silver boom). Covers the quarantine→conversion model (van Leur's "adhesion" critiqued); Pasai 1297 → Melaka springboard → 1400–1650 high tide; the militant anti-Portuguese sultanates (Aceh, Banten; Majapahit's fall c. 1527); the two missionary strategies (locally-married accommodating Muslim teachers with demanding markers — pork-renunciation, circumcision, "becoming Malay" = ethnic change — vs the celibate Iberian-defined Catholic priesthood destroying idols); the Malay Islamic literary flowering. Enriched `concepts/buddhism.md` (the Theravada sangha reform under royal control; sources_ingested→6) and `concepts/islam.md` (Islam in SE Asia; →4).

## 2026-06-12 section | Reid, Southeast Asia in the Age of Commerce, Vol. 2 | Section: Cycle 4 — Problems of the absolutist state (Ch IV) | Pages created: 1 | Pages updated: 1

Created `concepts/southeast-asian-absolutism.md` — Reid's analysis of the new gunpowder-fuelled centralising kingship and its rivals: the 13th-c. crisis of the classic temple-states → the port-states riding Ming trade (Ayutthaya's sakdi-na bureaucracy); the military revolution (firearms Chinese-invented & present before 1511, the European edge "never more than slight," the deep effect being to strengthen regimes over hinterlands); the great absolutists (Iskandar Muda of Aceh, Naresuan/Prasat Thong of Siam, Sultan Agung of Mataram cutting down the orang kaya); and the persistent contractual/conciliar rivals (the Melaka social contract, Makassar/Bugis federalism, the Banten/Banda/Ternate/Sulu councils). Foregrounds the central Reid–Lieberman debate: brittle commercial absolutism vs durable mainland administrative integration. Enriched `concepts/military-revolution.md` (the SE Asian test case; sources_ingested→3).

## 2026-06-12 section | Reid, Southeast Asia in the Age of Commerce, Vol. 2 | Section: Cycle 5 — The origins of Southeast Asian poverty (Ch V) + synthesis | Pages created: 1 | Pages updated: 2

Created `concepts/seventeenth-century-general-crisis.md` — Southeast Asia in the global general-crisis debate (the worldwide 1620–50 trade downturn; the silver contraction — Potosí after 1630, Japan's 1635 withdrawal & 1668 silver ban; the Ming collapse; the Little Ice Age; the Hobsbawm/Trevor-Roper/Goldstone/climate theories) — and Reid's case that SE Asia was hit hardest and most permanently, registering as a "retreat from the world economy" rather than population collapse, with the VOC as the crisis's winner; 1629 turning point. Enriched `processes/southeast-asian-age-of-commerce.md` (the crisis + the "origins of poverty" thesis + the permanent religious legacy; completed end-conditions) and `actors/dutch-east-india-company.md` (the VOC as the agent that ended the age of commerce — decentralisation-plus-cohesion, Banda 1621, Makassar 1669, the 1677 Mataram treaty; sources_ingested→3). Synthesis pass complete; Volume Synthesis Note written.

The conquest cycle of the colonial-LatAm large-volume ingest. Created `actors/pizarro-francisco` (the audacity-meets-collapse case in the Great-Man-vs-structure debate), `actors/las-casas-bartolome` (Protector of the Indians; the New Laws of 1542; the 1550–51 Valladolid debate vs. Sepúlveda; *Sublimis Deus* 1537), and promoted `controversies/black-legend` (the leyenda negra — Spanish-sourced via Las Casas, weaponized by England/Dutch; three positions on cruelty-vs-propaganda; open). ## 2026-06-13 section | Colonial Latin America (Burkholder & Johnson) | Section: Cycle 2 — The colonial state & economy (Chs 3–5) | Pages created: 4 | Pages updated: 4

The institutional/economic core of the volume. Created `processes/colonial-spanish-american-administration` (the Council of the Indies & House of Trade; the two viceroyalties; the audiencia as the key standing civil institution; corregidores/cabildos; the "wheels not pyramid" decentralization & *obedezco pero no cumplo*; above all the **sale of offices** 1557→1700 that creolized/"rooted" the bureaucracy and dropped royal control to its 1750 nadir — the premise of the Bourbon reforms; the slower, unsold Brazilian counterpart). Created `concepts/encomienda` (the grant of tribute/labor — not land; Caribbean catastrophe → Laws of Burgos 1512; the New Laws 1542 & Gonzalo Pizarro's revolt; decline at the cores, survival at peripheries) anchoring the full **labor sequence** — encomienda → repartimiento/mita → free wage labor/debt peonage → yanaconaje → Indian slavery (Brazil sugar, the bandeirantes) → repartimiento de bienes — with **flight** (forasteros/yanaconas) as indigenous agency. Created `places/potosi-and-colonial-silver` (the Cerro Rico 1545; mercury amalgamation & the Huancavelica monopoly as the master variable; Toledo's 1572 mita of ~13,500/yr; the human & mercury-poisoning cost; output vs Mexico; the city of 120,000). Created `processes/spiritual-conquest-and-colonial-church` (the patronato/padroado; the mendicants' & Jesuits' spiritual conquest, native-language evangelization, missions/reductions/aldeias & the Guaraní; Guadalupe/Tonantzin syncretism & failed native clergy; the Inquisition tribunals [Indians exempt]; the Church as the wealthiest institution & chief mortgage lender; nunneries; creolization; the Jesuit expulsion as the Bourbon turning point). Enriched `concepts/mita-and-andean-reciprocity` (Toledo's colonial mita & the demographic flight halving Upper Peru; sources→2), `processes/global-silver-trade` (the American supply side: Potosí/Mexican output, mercury monopolies, the Casa/consulado/fleet system, the Manila galleon, contraband; sources→2), `processes/virgin-soil-epidemics` (the serial colonial waves & region-varying nadir — Mexico c.1620s, Andes 1718–20 — Borah/Cook figures, the non-disease multipliers; sources→2), and `processes/atlantic-slave-trade` (the colonial-LatAm demand side: Brazil sugar/gold, 2.5M+ by 1810, the 2:1 sex ratio & lowland mortality; sources→6). Next: Cycle 3 — colonial society (Chs 6–8: the casta-system, the hacienda, family & daily life).

## 2026-06-13 section | Colonial Latin America (Burkholder & Johnson) | Section: Cycle 3 — Colonial society (Chs 6–8) | Pages created: 2 | Pages updated: 2

The social-history cycle. Created `concepts/casta-system` (the *sociedad de castas* — race ordered by **wealth, lineage, and culture as much as biology**, so the propertied "passed" as white; *limpieza de sangre* from religious → racial discrimination, claimed even by the surviving Inca/Tlaxcalan nobility; the mixed-caste vocabulary mestizo/mulatto-pardo/zambo/*casta*; casta paintings & sumptuary laws; the constrained lives of women & the family/marriage/dowry order; the Cope/Katzew/Martínez/Rappaport fluidity revisionism) and `processes/hacienda` (the great estate — Chevalier's "great hacienda" vs the market-business revisionism; its origins **not** from the encomienda but from land grants + the demographic collapse + urban/mining demand; debt peonage/inquilinaje/yanaconaje; the **symbiosis** of estate and impoverished free farmers who supplied harvest labor; the rural face of the casta hierarchy; survival to the 20th-c. land reforms). Enriched `processes/colonial-latin-america` (a "colonial society" section knitting together the administration/Church/labor/society pages) and `processes/atlantic-slave-trade` (colonial slave society: urban-vs-plantation conditions, manumission/self-purchase & the free-black populations, cofradías & African associations, flight & the Palmares quilombo). Next: Cycle 4 — reform, crisis & independence (Bourbon/Pombaline reforms, the 18th-c. economy, the wars of independence) + the volume synthesis.

## 2026-06-13 section | Colonial Latin America (Burkholder & Johnson) | Section: Cycle 4 — Reform, crisis & independence (later chapters) | Pages created: 2 | Pages updated: 1

The closing cycle. Created `processes/bourbon-reforms` (the 18th-c. Bourbon/Pombaline/Caroline drive to recentralize, tax & defend the empire — the secretary of state stripping the Council of the Indies; register ships & the Cádiz move; the **intendant system** (Gálvez); new viceroyalties (New Granada 1739, Río de la Plata 1776); expanded armies/militias; ***comercio libre*** 1778; **regalism & the Jesuit expulsion 1767**; Pombal's parallel Brazil reforms; the contradictory legacy — more revenue but **Creole alienation** + the Túpac Amaru/Comunero revolts of the 1780s — priming independence). Enriched `places/latin-america-modern` (linking the new colonial→independence pages into the modern arc; conquest → colony → independence → modern era; sources→2). Synthesis pass: Volume Synthesis Note written on the source page (the colonial order as process-not-plan; coercion+catastrophe, race-not-rigidly-biological society, the silver-and-slavery economy; the Bourbon-reform→independence causal chain; continuity into modern Latin America; Galeano's *Open Veins* flagged as the contesting counterpart still to ingest).

Enriched `processes/colonial-latin-america` ([CONTRADICTION] flag + dependency reading; sources→2), `places/potosi-and-colonial-silver` (Potosí as the emblem of "the open veins"; "Spain owned the cow, others drank the milk"; sources→2), `processes/plantation-economy` (Galeano's "King Sugar" monoculture-as-curse boom-and-bust; sources→2), and `places/latin-america-modern` (Galeano as the pure-external pole vs. Charlip's two-way model; sources→3). Theme: "our wealth has always generated our poverty by nourishing the prosperity of others" — silver→European capitalism (Marx's primitive accumulation), monoculture latifundia, and the contemporary IMF/multinational drain as one continuous chain. ## 2026-06-13 section | A History of Japan (Mason & Caiger) | Section: Cycle 3 — Early Modern Japan (Part IV, chs 12–14) | Pages created: 6 | Pages updated: 2

The reunification-and-Tokugawa cycle. Created the **three unifiers** — `actors/oda-nobunaga` ("Rule the Empire by Force"; Okehazama, the burning of Hieizan 1571, massed firearms at Nagashino 1575; made unification possible), `actors/toyotomi-hideyoshi` (the peasant-to-kampaku unifier; the land survey & the 1588 sword hunt that froze the samurai/farmer class line; the disastrous Korea invasions 1592/97; the start of Christian persecution), `actors/tokugawa-ieyasu` (Sekigahara 1600 → shogun 1603 → Osaka 1615; the patient founder of the 250-year peace). Created period pages `periods/azuchi-momoyama-period` (1568–1600 reunification; the gunpowder revolution; the breaking of militant Buddhism; Momoyama castle-and-gold-screen art & secularization; first Iberian contact) and `periods/edo-period` (1603–1868; the Tokugawa peace; the baku-han system — sankin kōtai, fudai/tozama; the bureaucratized stipendiary samurai; monetization & the commercial revolution; Edo/Osaka/Kyoto; the merchant rise & chōnin "floating world" culture — Saikaku, Chikamatsu, Kabuki/Bunraku; the rural landlord-entrepreneur class). Created `concepts/sakoku` (the 1639 closed-country policy after the Shimabara rebellion; Dutch at Dejima; the "never entirely closed" Korea/Ryūkyū/Ainu channels & equal-status diplomacy; the missed Scientific/Industrial Revolutions vs. the "developed in its own way, stayed cohesively Japanese" thesis; the Toby revisionism). Next: Cycle 4 — Modern Japan (Part V, chs 15–17: the Meiji Restoration & modernization; 1912–37 consensus-to-crisis; war in China, the Pacific War, the Occupation) + the volume synthesis.

## 2026-06-13 section | A Short Cultural History of Japan (Sansom) | Section: Cycle 2 — Heian court culture (Part III) | Pages created: 1 | Pages updated: 4

The classical-age cycle. Created `concepts/heian-court-culture` (the classical age of Japanese culture — the insulated aesthetic aristocracy for whom "aesthetic enjoyment was the chief business"; mono no aware; the kana-enabled women's literature [Genji, Pillow Book]; calligraphy as the master art & key to all Japanese aesthetics; the yamato-e picture-scrolls [the Genji scroll, Toba Sōjō]; shinden-zukuri & the Byōdōin Phoenix Hall; the Buddhist immanence/transience aesthetic — "a large mountain and a small philosopher"). Enriched `concepts/japanese-buddhism` (Sansom's depth on esoteric Tendai's eclecticism & Shingon's Tantric Dainichi/mantra/mandala under Kūkai; the Heian rise of Amidism — Kūya, Genshin's Ōjōyōshū, jiriki vs tariki, nembutsu; sources→3), `concepts/shinto` (Dual Shintō/Ryōbu — the phased Heian fusion via Shingon's ryōbu, the Shintō priesthood proposing identifications, honji suijaku crystallizing ~1100, but Ise's enduring anti-Buddhist taboos; sources→4), `periods/heian-period` (a court-culture section pointing to the new page; sources→2), and `actors/fujiwara-clan` (patrons of the classical age — Michinaga's salons, the Byōdōin, the Tosa style; sources→2). Next: Cycle 3 — the warrior age (Parts IV–V: Kamakura & Muromachi religion and arts — Zen, Noh, tea, ink painting, gardens).

## 2026-06-13 ingest-complete | A Short Cultural History of Japan (George B. Sansom, 1931/1952) | Total pages created: 3 | Total pages updated: 12 | Sections processed: 4

The **cultural-depth complement** to the Mason political spine for Japan (Phase B), processed via the Large-Volume Protocol in 4 cycles by the book's seven Parts. By design it created few new pages (3: `heian-court-culture`, `genroku-culture`, `tokugawa-intellectual-life`) and instead deepened the Mason-built period & religion pages (12 update-operations across 10 distinct pages: shinto ×2, nara-period, japanese-buddhism ×2, heian-period, fujiwara-clan, zen-buddhism, muromachi-period, kamakura-period, edo-period, sakoku). Sansom's thesis sharpens Mason's "borrow then domesticate" into the *friction* of assimilation ("outward form rather than inward significance"; "a conflict between native habits and alien principles") punctuated by genuine native breakthroughs (the Heian classical age; the Genroku townsman renaissance); the deep Buddhist imprint (immanence & transience) on Japanese aesthetics; religion fused with art and power (Dual Shintō; the Zen monasteries of the warrior age); and the Tokugawa intellectual prehistory (Neo-Confucianism, kokugaku, rangaku) of the Meiji Restoration. Filed to `raw/4. Modern Times/`. index.md banner + stats refreshed. **With Mason + Sansom the Japan gap is well filled (political spine + cultural body, prehistory→1867); Phase B remaining = The Tale of Genji (Waley) + the Cambridge History of Japan vols.** Git commit + push follow (source complete).

Next: Cycle 2 — the reform century & the Nara state (chs 3–4: Asuka enlightenment, the Great Reforms, the imperial/ritsuryō state, Nara & Tōdai-ji).

## 2026-06-13 section | Cambridge History of Japan Vol. 1 (Ancient Japan) | Section: Cycle 2 — The reform century & the Nara state (chs 3–4) | Pages created: 1 | Pages updated: 3

Created `concepts/ritsuryo-state` (the Chinese-modeled legal-bureaucratic state — *ritsu* penal + *ryō* administrative codes; the Ōmi→Asuka Kiyomihara→**Taihō 701**→Yōrō 757 sequence; Temmu's sacralized *tennō* & the 684 **kabane** "new nobility"; the unique Department of Worship ranking above the Council of State; ~66 provinces/~600 districts & the six-yearly census; the **handen** allotment land & **soyōchō** taxes + conscription; its erosion by shōen & Fujiwara familism — the formal name for the Nara/early-Heian centralized state). Enriched `events/taika-reform-645` (Inoue ch.3 — the **Four-Article Edict** of 646 [≈ the 1868 Charter Oath]; census/land survey, weapon collection, ban on private land sale; the **authenticity debate** [Tsuda] resolved as genuine, "midway between Shōtoku's 604 Injunctions and the Taihō code"; Temmu's post-Jinshin-war [672] consolidation; sources→2), `periods/nara-period` (the mature ritsuryō state; sources→3), and `actors/shotoku-taishi` (the Seventeen Injunctions of 604 as the opening term of the centralization arc; the "Asuka enlightenment"; sources→2). Next: Cycle 3 — the continent, kami & Buddha worship (chs 5–7).

The wiki's first **Cambridge reference volume on Japan** — the authoritative, interdisciplinary deepening of the ancient-Japan spine (Mason/Sansom/Kojiki/Genji). By design created just 1 page (`ritsuryo-state`) and deepened 8 existing (9 update-ops): early-agriculture-japan, yamato-japan, taika-reform-645, nara-period ×2, shotoku-taishi, shinto, japanese-buddhism, kojiki-yasumaro. Key contributions: a **less indigenous, more continental** ancient Japan (rice from the Yangtze delta; Shintō's pan-Asian/Pacific cosmos; ritsuryō Buddhism on the Tang/Silla model; the Yamato-Korea iron nexus + the **Mimana-colony revisionism**); the making of the **ritsuryō state** (Shōtoku 604 → Taika 646 → Taihō 701) and its erosion by the early shōen already in Nara; and Brown's **linealism/vitalism/optimism** account of why the sacred imperial line anchors Japanese historical consciousness. Holistic method (archaeology + texts + comparison), given presumptive weight where it refines Mason/Sansom. Filed to `raw/4. Modern Times/`. index banner+stats refreshed. **Phase B (Japan) remaining = Cambridge History of Japan Vols 3 (Medieval), 4 (Early Modern), 5 (19th c.), 6 (20th c.) + New CHJ 2.** Git commit + push follow (source complete).

## 2026-06-13 section | The Cambridge History of Japan, Vol. 3: Medieval Japan (Yamamura ed., 1990) | Section: Cycle 1 — The Kamakura order & its fall (Intro + chs 1–3) | Pages created: 1 | Pages updated: 4

Created `concepts/medieval-shoen` (the medieval estate system as socio-economic base: shōen vs. kokugaryō dual landholding [Ishii's Noto Province ōtabumi — 70% shōen]; the **shiki** rights hierarchy; the **jitō** warrior intrusion and the proprietors' *wayo* compromises — ***ukesho*** [fixed-tax contract, jitō total control] and ***shitaji chūbun*** [physical partition, maps with red lines]; Mass's caution that proprietors not jitō drove shitaji chūbun; partible→unitary inheritance concentrating warrior power). Enriched `periods/kamakura-period` (Mass — the bakufu as a **court of justice**: Yasutoki's *rensho*/*hyōjōshū*, the Jōei Code 1232 [procedure/precedent/appeal], the **anti-conciliar revisionism** [Goble vs. Satō — hyōjōshū "a vehicle of the regent"]; the **Jōkyū War 1221** turning point [~3,000 estates confiscated, Rokuhara tandai]; the Mongol-war fiscal slide → tokuso autocracy from 1285 → 1333; sources→3), `concepts/shogunate-bakufu` (the Kamakura bakufu as a **judicial state** — hyōjōshū/hikitsuke/osso bugyō; Mass's revisionism; tokuso/miuchibito autocracy; sources→2), `actors/minamoto-no-yoritomo` (the **shugo & jitō** as his durable legacy — the legal machinery of warrior landholding; jitō as the bakufu courts' chief object; sources→2), `events/mongol-invasions-of-japan-1274-1281` (Ishii — the fiscal aftermath in detail: **no reward land**, impoverished gokenin [partible inheritance + cash economy + war costs], the **tokusei debt-cancellation decrees** [1284], absorption by shugo/miuchibito/akutō → Go-Daigo & the fall; sources→2).

## 2026-06-13 section | The Cambridge History of Japan, Vol. 3: Medieval Japan (Yamamura ed., 1990) | Section: Cycle 2 — The Muromachi order (chs 4–6) | Pages created: 1 | Pages updated: 4

Created `concepts/shugo-daimyo` (the provincial constable → territorial lord: the limited Kamakura police-office vs. its Muromachi transformation; **Hall's rehabilitation** of the Ashikaga bakufu [Yoshimitsu/Yoshinori held all secular authority; the rotating **kanrei** deputyship; strong shugo *aided* the bakufu under shogunal primacy]; **Imatani** on the powers that made shugo regional hegemons — ***hanzei*** [half of shōen dues], ***tansen*** [provincewide tax], land-dispute enforcement, hereditary office; the **kubō/tandai** peripheral failures; the **Nagahara-vs-Kurokawa** shugo-vs-kokujin debate; Ōnin → *gekokujō* → sengoku daimyo). Enriched `periods/muromachi-period` (Hall's reassessment of Ashikaga efficiency & its diverse fiscal base [Ming tribute trade, za guilds, tolls]; Imatani on shugo/kokujin decentralization; sources→3), `concepts/shogunate-bakufu` (Muromachi bakufu reassessed — kanrei, shugo as asset not threat), `actors/samurai` (the institutional ladder jitō→kokujin-ryōshu / shugo→shugo-domain; sources→2), `concepts/medieval-shoen` (Nagahara's three pivotal causes of the shōen's demise — jitō usurpation, shugo *hanzei* absorption, and village-level change: autonomous ***sō*** communities + the **1428/1441 *ikki*** [the 1441 league forcing a *tokusei* edict]).

## 2026-06-13 section | The Cambridge History of Japan, Vol. 3: Medieval Japan (Yamamura ed., 1990) | Section: Cycle 3 — Medieval economy & East Asia (chs 7–9) | Pages created: 1 | Pages updated: 3

Created `processes/medieval-japanese-commerce` (Kozo Yamamura's "The Growth of Medieval Commerce" — the commercialization of Japan c. 1250–1550: rising agricultural productivity, urban growth, **za** guilds, bills of exchange, the spread of **imported Chinese coin** to rural cultivators, **commutation** of dues to cash; the "money without a mint" puzzle [why the Ashikaga/shugo daimyo used Chinese rather than minting their own]; conflicts over *za*, *tokuseirei*, and dues among estate elite/warriors/commoners; the bridge to the early-modern economy). Enriched `concepts/medieval-shoen` (Nagahara's "The Medieval Peasant" — the shōen *created* the medieval peasantry; growing *sō* village autonomy [self-governance, welfare, taxation]; everyday peasant food/clothing/shelter), `periods/muromachi-period` (Yamamura commerce + **Kawazoe**'s four reasons for Yoshimitsu's entry into the **Ming tribute system** [art-income, legitimacy, security, control of the Kyūshū tandai]; the Southern Court blocking the dazaifu until 1392; Korea/Ryūkyū/**wakō**), `events/mongol-invasions-of-japan-1274-1281` (Kawazoe — the Mongols' aim of isolating the Southern Sung via Koryŏ; the **three reasons** Japan refused: read as a war-declaration, Southern-Sung-biased intelligence, warrior-society temperament).

## 2026-06-13 ingest-complete | The Cambridge History of Japan, Vol. 3: Medieval Japan (Yamamura ed., 1990) | Total pages created: 4 | Total pages updated: 8 | Sections processed: 4

The authoritative Cambridge reference on **medieval Japan (1185–1603)** — the warrior age from the Kamakura bakufu through the Muromachi/Ashikaga order and into the *sengoku* eve of unification. Created 4 pages — **medieval-shoen** (the estate system: shiki hierarchy, jitō encroachment via *ukesho*/*shitaji chūbun*, the Nagahara dissolution), **shugo-daimyo** (constable → territorial lord: *hanzei*/*tansen*, Hall's bakufu rehabilitation, the shugo-vs-kokujin debate), **medieval-japanese-commerce** (Yamamura's commercial revolution: monetization on Chinese coin, *za* guilds, money-without-a-mint), and the controversy **zen-and-medieval-japanese-aesthetics** (Varley/Ruch vs. Sansom/Mason) — and deepened 8: kamakura-period, muromachi-period, shogunate-bakufu, minamoto-no-yoritomo, mongol-invasions-of-japan-1274-1281, samurai, zen-buddhism, japanese-buddhism. Through-line: the **transfer of power over the land from capital proprietors to warriors** — institutional (Mass on the Kamakura judicial state & Hōjō revisionism; the Jōkyū War 1221; Hall's Ashikaga rehabilitation), economic (the shōen's dissolution + commercialization), diplomatic (Kawazoe — the Mongol war breaking Kamakura; the Ming tribute system), and cultural (Ōsumi's Kamakura Buddhist revolution; Varley/Ruch's anti-Zen-centric revision of medieval aesthetics).
